# Threat model

This document names who can act on ExecBound, what each trust boundary must hold, which threats apply at each boundary, and what evidence shows that a mitigation works. It maps attacker goals to the [security invariants](#security-invariants) below, the code that enforces them and the acceptance scenarios in the [acceptance inventory](ACCEPTANCE_TESTS.md) (A to X), the [context contracts guide](CONTEXT_CONTRACTS.md) (CC1 to CC12, CP1 to CP12 and CA1 to CA10) and the shared limit scopes (SL1 to SL12), external execution checkpoint (GC1 to GC18), Arcade logic extension (AL1 to AL34), sample targets (SM1 to SM11), denial notice (DN1 to DN10), action listing (LA1 to LA8), self-hosted export (EX5 and EX8 to EX10) and security signals (SG1 to SG17) sections of the inventory. Residual risks are stated plainly, each with the guide, row or test that states it. The [architecture guide](ARCHITECTURE.md) describes the components; this document describes how they fail and why they should not.

## Security invariants

These hold on every path ExecBound enforces, and each threat below is a way of attacking one of them.

- An agent's identity and tenant come from authentication, and every object and connector account is scoped to a tenant.
- Unknown or mismatched action or target input is rejected.
- A failure of required context, policy, state or audit intent prevents consequential dispatch.
- A hard denial always wins over permits and approvals.
- Approvals are scoped, expiring and held server-side, and each is consumed by one execution claim.
- Target impact is shared across agents, runs and adapters within the configured principal or tenant scope.
- The provider checks the frozen precondition atomically where the vendor offers a conditional write. Where it offers none, what is proved is ExecBound's own read and comparison immediately before the write, with the window between them measured and retained, not atomicity.
- An uncertain operation cannot be retried or refunded without sufficient evidence.
- Replay never executes a provider or changes live budgets, approvals or execution state.
- Human pages treat agent and provider strings as untrusted data.
- Raw provider credentials, runtime configuration, signing material and control stores stay outside the protected agent's read and write authority, and there are no generic passthrough tools or agent-controlled connector destinations.

## Scope and method

Covered: the gateway (HTTP and MCP), the human surface, PostgreSQL, the protected mock providers, monitoring intake and alert delivery, security signal intake and the operator's mapping of it, owner tooling and the CLI, offline replay and export, the build and CI pipeline, and the export of the self-hosted source to its public repository.

Not covered: real vendor systems beyond the one real binding, Microsoft Graph's user disable, which T3.14 to T3.16 cover (every other binding is a controlled mock provider), operating-system sandboxing of the gateway process, the platform a deployment runs on (trusted as described in [DEPLOYMENT.md](DEPLOYMENT.md)), and the security of a customer's own agent platform such as a Tines tenant. B9 below covers what a Gate caller can do **to ExecBound**; what it does at its own vendor with its own credential is outside this model entirely, which is why that boundary's rows are mostly limits rather than controls.

Method: for each trust boundary, threats are classified with STRIDE (Spoofing, Tampering, Repudiation, Information disclosure, Denial of service, Elevation of privilege). Each row records the mitigation, where it lives in code, the scenario or test that exercises it, and a status:

| Status | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Verified | An acceptance scenario or named test exercises the mitigation on real PostgreSQL, real Chromium or the real Cedar engine. |
| Partial | A control exists, but a documented gap remains. |
| Open | No control yet. |
| Accepted | A design limit stated in the documentation; not scheduled for change. |

## Assets

- **Execution authority**: the ability to make a provider perform a consequential action. Only the durable execution claim inside one tenant transaction confers it.
- **Approvals**: scoped, expiring, server-side human decisions consumed with exactly one execution claim.
- **Impact budgets and target claims**: shared limits per principal, tenant, principal group, connector account and resource selector, and one unresolved claim per canonical target.
- **Frozen plans and terminal evidence**: the resolved request, plan hash, provider journals and audit events that replay and reconciliation rely on.
- **Audit chain**: per-tenant hash-chained events with a separately retained trusted head.
- **Credentials and keys**: agent tokens, human sessions and CSRF tokens (stored only as hashes), an administrator's personal access tokens and their backing credentials (stored only as hashes, the backing credential minted and revoked with the token), continuation signing keys, provider dispatch and control credentials, provider evidence keys, monitoring source tokens, security signal producer tokens (stored only as digests the runtime role cannot read) and the database roles.
- **Tenant isolation**: every tenant table is tenant-scoped with forced row-level security, and the account tables are fenced by account the same way.
- **Policy bundles and context settings**: immutable, digest-bound content whose activation is an audited ADMIN action.
- **Monitoring findings and alerts**: detective evidence that must stay separate from execution authority.
- **Runtime configuration**: private files that name connector destinations, keys and precondition classes; never derived from a request.
- **Executor keys**: per-tenant Ed25519 public keys an external executor's report is verified against. ExecBound holds no private half, so the asset is the *ring's integrity* — which keys are registered, when each began and whether one was retired or cut off — rather than a secret at rest.

## Actors

| Actor | May | Must not | Source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Protected agent | Propose one bounded action and target with its own credential; read its own execution status; list the operations its own space offers. | Supply trusted facts, approvals, destinations or passthrough arguments; read provider credentials, configuration, signing material or control stores. | [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md) trust boundaries; [SURFACES.md](SURFACES.md) |
| Gate caller | Propose one bounded action and target exactly as a protected agent does; receive a grant for an operation an owner-provisioned mapping marked externally executed; act at its **own** vendor with its **own** credential; report the outcome once, with the report secret that grant returned. | Select Gate, name an executor, set a precondition class or promote an evidence tier — all four are owner-provisioned bundle content or owner-registered configuration; settle any execution but the one its grant names; settle an operation ExecBound dispatched; release its own reservation or free its own target by assertion; reconcile, approve or dispatch through ExecBound. | [CHECKPOINT.md](CHECKPOINT.md) |
| Control point | Call `POST /pre` and `POST /post` with an ExecBound agent credential held in its own configuration; carry a tool identity, the declared inputs, reported metadata and an end-user string; receive one of three enumerated codes. | Select which declaration applies, choose an action, a target or an incident, move a tool between governed and ungoverned, set a precondition class or promote a tier — all of that is the operator's map or the frozen plan; supply an identity or a budget scope; settle any execution but the one its own correlation row names; release a claim or refund a reservation by anything it says; correct a settlement it made. | [ARCADE.md](ARCADE.md) |
| Human approver | Sign in for a session that ends after 15 minutes idle and 8 hours at most; approve or deny inside an owner-assigned target and action scope. | Reconcile; decide outside the assigned scope; dispatch anything by approving. | [SURFACES.md](SURFACES.md); [EXECUTION.md](EXECUTION.md) |
| ADMIN | View tenant activity; reconcile with a reason and terminal evidence; export audit history; view monitoring pages and acknowledge findings; activate bundles; issue and revoke agent and approver credentials and manage approver assignments through the administration functions. | Stand in for an assigned approver; settle an uncertain operation without proof; create or change tenants or the registry. Administrators are made and unmade only by role grants, bounded as T5.28 says. | [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md); [OPERATIONS.md](OPERATIONS.md) |
| Owner tooling | Write tenancy, credentials, mappings, inventory, sources, aliases, canonical identity, approval assignments and `resource_facts` with the owner role; the `execbound` administration commands cover tenants, principals, credentials, approver assignments and principal groups, and the `context-source-*` and `context-import` commands cover context sources, inventory facts and incidents from adapter-produced manifests validated against the contract catalog; every change is audited under an ADMIN actor. | Be reachable from any HTTP route; run with the runtime role. | [EXECUTION.md](EXECUTION.md); [CONTEXT_CONTRACTS.md](CONTEXT_CONTRACTS.md) |
| Protected mock provider | Accept a fixed configured account and frozen command; mutate atomically against an expected version; sign journals; fence late arrivals with a cancellation tombstone. | Accept agent credentials; expose provisioning, reset or passthrough routes. | [PROVIDER.md](PROVIDER.md); [API_REFERENCE.md](API_REFERENCE.md) |
| PostgreSQL | Hold all gateway authority, execution state, impact, approvals and audit events under forced row-level security. | Be reached with the runtime role by anything but trusted gateway code. | [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md); [DATA_MODEL.md](DATA_MODEL.md) |
| Monitoring source | Post allowlisted story metadata with a source token bound to one tenant, source and story. | Authorize execution, create approvals, settle effects or change limits. | [TINES_MONITORING.md](TINES_MONITORING.md) |
| Security signal producer | Post `execbound.security-signal.v1` envelopes inside its owner-registered scope with an `sg1.` token bound to one tenant and one source. | Read anything, see a decision, approve, dispatch, reserve impact, change policy or mappings, be called by ExecBound, or make its verdict mean anything the bundle's mapping does not say. | [SIGNALS.md](SIGNALS.md) |
| Notification receiver | Receive authenticated at-least-once deliveries carrying only identifiers and a rule version. | Be chosen by a finding or an event; trigger any protected resend. | [TINES_MONITORING.md](TINES_MONITORING.md#intake-evaluation-and-delivery-contract) |
| CI | Run the full suite and both demonstrations on the exact source head and publish a manifest bound to that commit. | Replace security tests with documentation checks. | `scripts/ci.py`; [ACCEPTANCE_TESTS.md](ACCEPTANCE_TESTS.md) |
| Public contributor | Open a pull request on the public repository, whose CI runs on GitHub-hosted runners with a token that only reads. | Run code on the maintainers' own machines; reach the credential that writes the public repository; change the development repository or the hosted service except through a maintainer's review. | The public repository's `CONTRIBUTING.md` |

## Trust boundaries

| ID | Boundary | What must hold |
| --- | --- | --- |
| B1 | Protected agent to gateway (HTTP and MCP) | Identity comes only from an authenticated credential; every input is untrusted until validated; nothing an agent sends can grant authority. |
| B2 | Gateway to PostgreSQL | Trusted code sets transaction-local tenant context; runtime privileges are restricted; every consequential write commits atomically under the tenant fence. |
| B3 | Gateway to protected provider | Only a configured account and frozen command can dispatch; only authenticated, command-bound evidence can settle. |
| B4 | Human browser to human surface | A current session, exact origin, CSRF token, signed state, assigned scope and current role are all required for a decision. |
| B5 | Owner tooling and ADMIN to authority data | Registry, policy and trusted facts change only through owner or audited ADMIN paths. |
| B6 | Monitoring sources and receivers | Source-reported observations stay outside execution authority; destinations are operator-configured. |
| B7 | Live system to replay and export | Export needs current ADMIN authority; replay has no live capability. |
| B8 | Build, dependencies and CI | Locked, hash-pinned environments; evidence bound to the tested commit. |
| B9 | Gate caller to gateway, and its vendor beyond it | Placement, the executor's identity, the precondition class and the evidence tier are all owner-provisioned or owner-registered; a grant reserves before it is issued and settles exactly one execution once; nothing an executor says releases a canonical claim or refunds a reservation. What happens at the vendor is outside ExecBound entirely, and no row here claims otherwise. |
| B10 | Control point to hook surface, and the correlation table behind it | The tool map is deployment configuration and no hook field selects it; the extension translates and calls the same kernel methods an agent reaches, so nothing it does can permit what the kernel did not; a pre hook that answers `OK` on a governed decision has reserved and claimed first; a post hook settles at most the one execution its own correlation row names, at tier `ASSERTED` and never higher, and neither of its two statements refunds anything. The correlation row holds a **usable** report secret while its grant is live, which is a new trust boundary rather than an inherited one: a reader of that table can close a row and let the scheduled run free its target, and can never reduce what the tenant is charged. |
| B11 | Console to model provider | The assistant reads only through the asking person's session, writes nothing, sends nothing secret, and every answer is text whose links are only to ids a tool returned; the feature is absent without its block and off per tenant until an ADMIN turns it on. |
| B12 | Development repository to its public export | Only what the export manifest names leaves, and nothing that leaves links back into the development repository or carries a secret, a private name or the hosted service's address without a recorded reason; a fix leaves only once the hosted service runs it; the public repository's CI never runs on the maintainers' own machines, and code contributed there reaches the development repository only through a maintainer's review. |
| B13 | Security signal producer to gateway, and the mapping behind it | A producer's token opens `/v1/signals` and nothing else, and one fenced function authenticates it and checks its capability, subject, producer and times against the source's owner-registered scope; what a verdict means is the operator's mapping in the bundle, re-applied at evaluation to the records the plan froze; absence, staleness, revocation and a foreign space are all `INVALID_CONTEXT`, which denies, and `clean` needs every source it rests on to attest liveness. |

## Threats and mitigations

### B1: Protected agent to gateway

| ID | STRIDE | Threat | Mitigation | Evidence | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| T1.1 | S | Forged or guessed agent credential | Opaque tokens; the SHA-256 digest is compared in constant time even when no row exists; expiry and revocation are checked in the lookup (`identity.py`). | K; `tests/test_runtime.py::test_http_foreign_status_and_bad_identity_are_opaque` | Verified |
| T1.2 | S | MCP client metadata or a cached session used as identity | Stateless transport; every `tools/call` authenticates; metadata never establishes identity (`surfaces/mcp.py`). | M; `tests/test_mcp_surface.py`, `tests/test_identity_surfaces.py` | Verified |
| T1.3 | T | Caller claims replace trusted facts such as tier, incident or environment | Claims are selectors only; facts come from the owner-provisioned registry and contract sources (`resolution/`). | E, T, CC6; `tests/test_policy_evaluation.py::test_caller_claim_changes_cannot_change_cedar_entities` | Verified |
| T1.4 | T | Unknown operation, passthrough arguments, multiple or embedded targets | Strict wire models with forbidden extras and bounded patterns (`requests.py`); unknown mappings rejected before admission; an operation outside the agent's space is refused the same way (T1.17). | F, Q; `tests/test_requests.py`, `tests/test_resolution.py::test_unknown_operation_fails_without_foreign_disclosure` | Verified |
| T1.5 | E | Agent supplies an approval flag or approval identifier | No request schema accepts approval evidence; approvals are server-side rows consumed inside the execution claim (`policy/approval.py`, `execution/`). | C, G, R | Verified |
| T1.6 | E | Agent calls human, administrative or reconciliation routes | Rejected before any provider network activity and audited as a rejection. The served routes are asserted against a declared authority map, so a route added later fails the suite until it is classified, and **every** route needing a session is exercised with an agent credential rather than a sample of five. Re-examined when the external execution checkpoint added one agent route: `POST /v1/executions/{id}/outcome` takes the same AGENT authentication and is **the first agent-authenticated route that settles an execution** — before it none did, and a dispatch ticket is a secret internal capability an agent never holds ([ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md)), so nothing about this is a capability an agent already had. What bounds it is the list that follows and not an equivalence. It refuses an execution whose frozen mapping does not declare an external executor **before** the report secret is compared, so a dispatched operation is unreportable whether or not the secret matches; it refuses another principal's execution as `NOT_FOUND`; and the connector-free administrator exit is reachable only from an ADMIN session, never from this route. The act grant is not an authenticator here and cannot be added to a report, because the body forbids extra fields. | K, GC9, GC15; `tests/test_route_authority.py`, `tests/test_human_surface.py::test_agent_bearer_cannot_use_human_routes`, `tests/test_protected_path.py::test_agent_reconciliation_rejected_before_provider_network`, `tests/test_checkpoint.py::test_gc9_every_report_the_route_refuses_leaves_the_row_and_the_secret_where_they_were` | Verified |
| T1.7 | T | Idempotency key replayed with a changed payload, or raced across adapters | Same-key conflicts; one pending approval per racing first intent; precommit failures roll back every claim. | H, V; `tests/test_execution.py::test_same_key_changed_intent_conflicts_under_race`, `::test_precommit_failures_roll_back_every_claim` | Verified |
| T1.8 | I | Reading another tenant's or another agent's execution | Status requires the owning agent; foreign identifiers and bad identities return opaque responses. | S; opaque status test above | Verified |
| T1.9 | I | Engine, provider or database diagnostics leak through errors | Unexpected exceptions map to a generic 503 (`app.py`); Cedar and provider diagnostics are never echoed. | N; `tests/test_cedar.py` | Verified |
| T1.10 | D | Request flood exhausts admission | A process-wide in-flight limiter (32) returns 429; fixed-window limits per authenticated credential (shared by HTTP and MCP) and per direct peer address refuse with `RATE_LIMITED` before parsing or admission (`surfaces/limits.py`). | `tests/test_rate_limits.py::test_agent_requests_beyond_the_credential_limit_are_refused_then_reset`, `::test_refused_execute_requests_consume_nothing` | Verified |
| T1.11 | R | Failed authentication leaves no durable record | Every surface records a bounded per-minute counter in `authentication_failures` by surface and reason, attributed only to a credential or monitoring source that authentication verified exists; responses are unchanged, recording is best effort and audited history is untouched (`identity.py`). | `tests/test_authentication_failures.py` (AF1 to AF14) | Verified, see R8 |
| T1.12 | S | Guessed, replayed or brute-forced sandbox invitation code on the public `/onboard` page | A code is `ebi1.<tenant>.<invitation>.<43-character secret>`; only its SHA-256 is stored and the database compares it under a row lock inside the `onboarding_claim` definer function, which also requires the slot to be unclaimed and the invitation unexpired and unredeemed, so a replay fails on `redeemed_at`. Five secret mismatches revoke the invitation server-side, the count committed before the refusal; the page adds a per-selector window shared with sign-in and the per-peer window, and refuses a forged CSRF field, a bearer header, a wrong origin, an extra field or a query string before the database. Every refusal is one sentence; the code and the selector reach only the structured log (`onboarding.refused`). A code sent by email is minted inside the send and exists only in memory and the one message, travels only over verified TLS, and its invitation is revoked unless the relay accepted the message, so a code from an uncertain attempt never works. | hosted: SO3, SO4, SO10, SO11, SO11b | Verified |
| T1.13 | T | An agent retries a completed action under a new idempotency key and acts twice, consuming impact a second time | A caller that has no key management sends `reference`, the ticket, case or run id, and the gateway derives the key server-side with `requests.derive_key` byte for byte as the client's `intent_key` does, so one ticket lands on one intent whichever surface carries it and a retry through either progresses it; `ActionRequest` accepts exactly one of `reference` and `idempotency_key`, so a caller cannot send both and choose which applies. | `tests/test_requests.py::test_reference_derives_the_key_exactly_as_the_client_does`, `tests/test_requests.py::test_reference_and_key_are_exactly_one`, `tests/test_mcp_surface.py::test_the_same_reference_over_http_and_mcp_is_one_intent` | Verified |
| T1.14 | T | An agent names or infers a space, and acts on another customer's objects through a mapping that is not its own | No request field, header or credential attribute carries a space: the space is read from the agent's own `principals` row inside the fence (`spaces.agent_space`), and a mapping whose `connector_accounts` row belongs to another space and is not offered tenant-wide is refused before any provider contact: by admission's offered check, before arguments are validated or a target is read (`execution/offered.py`), or, for a request held for approval and re-sent unchanged after its account or agent left the space, by `_account_in_space` (`execution/admission.py`). The audit records `INVALID_CONFIGURATION`; the agent is answered 503 `EXECUTION_UNAVAILABLE`, exactly as for an unknown operation, and never `INVALID_CONFIGURATION` (T1.17). The active bundle is the **space's** selection, so a space with none fails closed exactly as a tenant with none did. | SP1, SP5, LA4; `tests/test_spaces_kernel.py`, `tests/test_action_listing.py` | Verified |
| T1.15 | D | An agent of an archived space keeps working | "Takes no new work" has one meaning and is checked where the emergency stop is checked: admission refuses before any dispatch, audits the rejection, and answers 423 `SPACE_ARCHIVED` in the stop's own shape. An archived space stays fully readable and loses no history, and a `DISPATCH_COMMITTED` grant already issued is not recalled, exactly as the stop does not recall one. | SP9; `tests/test_spaces_kernel.py` | Verified |
| T1.16 | I | An agent learns the policy from its own denials: which rule matched, which fact put a target out of bounds, which budget is spent, or what a reviewer wrote | A `DENIED` answer, over HTTP and MCP, carries the view's own members, `denial` and `notice`, one fixed sentence built from the request's reference and the category alone (`runtime.denial_notice`, added by `Runtime.present` for that state and no other); `ExecutionView` has no reasons field, and the recorded reason, the rules a denial matched, the facts they read and any reviewer comment reach people only, on the record a person finds by searching the console for the same reference. The sentence promises only what holds for every cause, that re-sending the same request returns the same answer, and never calls a refusal permanent: a new request can be decided differently once a busy target is released, a budget's window moves on, a policy or fact changes or a person is asked again. The rule and why: the protected agent is the governed party and may be mistaken, prompt-injected or compromised, so it is told a denial's state and reference and never its cause beyond the two categories below, and gets no map of the policy beyond the decision itself; a reviewer's words stay with people. The `execute` tool description, the agent guide, the agent recipes and the client's tool examples tell it to stop and relay the reference rather than guess. A busy target is answered the same way, as a `DENIED` with reason `RESOURCE_BUSY` and `denial` `busy`, and is no longer published as a retryable 409. **Observe mode is a deliberate exception**: an `OBSERVED` answer, from an observing tenant or from an observe-only mapping in an enforcing one, carries `observed.reasons`, rule ids included (`HARD_LIMIT:<rule id>`), because observe mode exists to show a tenant what enforcement would do and nothing runs under it. What stays visible: the decision itself, so an agent that tries many targets still learns which it may act on; and whether a person was involved, because the view's `approval_state` is `DENIED` after a reviewer refused, `INVALIDATED` when a request that held an approval was then denied, and null when the first decision denied it. The reviewer's comment never reaches the agent. **Categories, the one extension**: a `DENIED` answer also carries `denial`, which `store.denial_category` reads from the recorded `DENIED` transition, so the first answer, a re-send and a status read agree byte for byte. It is `busy` exactly when the recorded reason is `RESOURCE_BUSY` alone, `limit` whenever a `HARD_LIMIT:<rule id>` is among the recorded reasons, whatever sits beside it, `NOT_AUTHORIZED` included (decided on September 28, 2026), and `withheld` for every other denial; `notice` is the category's fixed sentence, and the limit's promises only that a new request after the limit resets is decided afresh, which stays true when a rule denies the request too. This closes a probe: under "every recorded reason is a limit", a spent limit made `limit` mean "the policy allows this target" and `withheld` mean "a rule denies it", a per-target verdict with nothing run and no reviewer asked. That no longer holds because `policy/evaluate.py` checks every limit rule against the request once the Cedar policy sets are evaluated, whatever their verdict, and records each applicable spent one beside `NOT_AUTHORIZED` or `POLICY_ERROR`; only a refusal that stops evaluation before the policy sets (the identity, the bundle, the arguments, the trusted entities or the usage snapshot) or an unexpected evaluation failure (`DEPENDENCY_UNAVAILABLE`) records no limit, and those are `withheld` and carry no policy verdict either. What each category discloses, and nothing more: `busy` tells the agent that another request holds the target and, because the busy check runs after the policy decision (`execution/admission.py`), that the policy otherwise allowed this request. `limit` tells it that a shared limit covering this request is spent; for a limit scoped to the principal, its space, the tenant or the agent's own groups that is all, and for a limit scoped by selector or by account it is also an inference about a fact, that the target matches the selector or belongs to the account. It carries no per-target policy verdict. `withheld` tells it nothing beyond the decision. No category names the rule, the limit, its window, a count, a fact or the other execution. `busy` and a tenant-wide `limit` are the cross-space channels residual risk [R24](#residual-risks) accepts, a target another space holds and a tenant limit another space spent, now stated to the agent rather than left for it to infer; they still say nothing about which space or what. Accepted because an honest agent needs to know when waiting is worth a new request, and both facts are learnable anyway by trying again later, when a busy target is free or a limit's window has moved on. **Refusals before any plan** tell the agent no more than before: an unknown target, a claim the inventory does not hold, a run that is not live and stale context are all answered the same 503 `EXECUTION_UNAVAILABLE`, and the cause the audit records is read from two columns of the one coherent snapshot statement, so every refusal after the mapping read costs the same statements whatever its cause (DN8). Only the audit's `execution.rejected` event names the cause (`INVALID_OPERATION`, `INVALID_TARGET`, `INVALID_CONTEXT` and the rest), from the closed list `RESOLUTION_CODES`, so no caller string becomes a reason. Two further oracles, bad arguments answered 400 or 503 by whether the operation exists in the tenant and the agent-facing `INVALID_CONFIGURATION`, are closed by T1.17. | DN1 to DN10, OB3; `tests/test_denial_notice.py`, `tests/test_denial_categories.py`, `tests/test_observe_mode.py::test_ob2_ob3_would_be_eligible_and_denied_requests_are_observed_with_their_reasons` | Verified |
| T1.17 | I | An agent maps what exists beyond its own space: which operations the tenant maps, which another space offers, where an operation is placed, or which targets exist | **Discovery, of its own space alone.** `GET /v1/actions` and the MCP tool `list_actions` answer what the agent's own space offers (`execution/offered.py`): each `operation_ref`, its catalog action and label, the argument names and the values or range each accepts, the target kinds it accepts and whether an incident is required. They never list a target, a rule, a limit, a placement, an account, a mapping or another space's operations; an observe-only or checkpoint operation is listed exactly as a connected one is, because the agent may send both and placement is never disclosed. A listed action is not permission: every request is still decided. Listed does not mean connected either: the listing reads no connector configuration, so a listed operation whose account has no connector still answers 503 `CONNECTOR_UNAVAILABLE`, which concerns only the agent's own space. An archived space answers 423 `SPACE_ARCHIVED`, a space with no active policy lists nothing, and an emergency stop does not stop a listing, as it does not stop a status read. A refused listing is audited as the surface's refusal (`operation` `surface`), as a refused status read is, and the runtime logs `actions.listed` with the count alone. A `tools/call` may leave `arguments` out, as MCP allows and the SDK's own client does for a tool called with none; when it is sent it must be an object, and `list_actions` refuses any argument. **Refusal, alike for everything outside the space.** Admission checks the requested `operation_ref` against the same one-statement read before it validates arguments or reads a target, for a new key and for a changed request under a known key. An operation the tenant does not map, one it maps but the space's bundle does not name, one whose mapping the catalog no longer binds, one the bundle names through another space's account and one the bundle names at a reference the tenant's mapping no longer matches (a stale policy, as a sample removal and reinstall leaves one) are all refused exactly as an unknown operation is: the same 503 `EXECUTION_UNAVAILABLE`, the same statements in the same order, and no execution record. That closes both oracles T1.16 recorded, bad arguments answered 400 or 503 by whether the operation exists anywhere in the tenant and the agent-facing `INVALID_CONFIGURATION`; bad arguments are refused 400 only for an operation the space offers. Only the audit tells the causes apart: `INVALID_OPERATION` for the first three, `INVALID_CONFIGURATION` for another space's account and `INVALID_BUNDLE` for a stale reference, which wins when the reference is both stale and on another space's account. The audit's `INVALID_BUNDLE` therefore now covers two agent answers, 409 from a checkpoint mapping on a connected account or a key ring that cannot attest a grant, and 503 for a stale reference. `INVALID_CONFIGURATION` is never answered to an agent wherever admission raises it (`AUDIT_ONLY`): the offered check, `_account_in_space` for a held request re-sent unchanged after its account or agent left the space, and an agent in more than 64 groups. **What changed with it**, decided on September 28, 2026: an operation outside the space no longer leaves a `DENIED` record with reason `INVALID_BUNDLE`, which was itself a sign that the operation existed, and observe mode no longer records an `OBSERVED` row for one; a new key for an intent still pending approval whose operation the space no longer offers is refused 503 rather than attached to the pending row; and the evaluator's own `INVALID_BUNDLE` for a bundle reference that does not match the plan's mapping is practically unreachable for a new or changed request, because the offered check compares the same reference first, and stays in `policy/evaluate.py` as the defence it was. Targets are never listed. | LA1 to LA8; `tests/test_action_listing.py` | Verified |
| T1.18 | D | Recording an agent key's last use slows, blocks or changes authentication, or is written for a key that did not authenticate | `credential_used` (0079) is a definer function under the tenant fence; the runtime holds EXECUTE on it and no `UPDATE` on `credentials`. `identity.authenticate` calls it for an agent's key alone (a person's or an owner tool's credential is never stamped, so a read-only owner command writes nothing), at most once a minute per key, after every check has passed, never on a refusal (a refusal raises inside the authentication transaction, which rolls back) and never in the decision transaction. It runs inside a savepoint whose errors are swallowed, so the identity is returned exactly as it would be without it. The function stamps nothing rather than failing when the application's clock that issued the key is ahead of the database's, and takes the row with `FOR NO KEY UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, which does not conflict with the key-share locks execution, approval and session inserts take through their foreign keys and never waits on a revocation's lock. Nothing authorises, expires or revokes by the column; it is display and notice state only. | AK2 to AK4; `tests/test_key_last_used.py::test_ak2_a_stamp_that_raises_never_changes_authentication`, `::test_ak3_a_share_lock_from_an_open_execution_does_not_delay_the_stamp`, `::test_ak3_a_locked_key_authenticates_at_once_without_a_stamp`, `::test_ak4_the_migration_adds_both_columns_and_three_functions_and_the_downgrade_removes_them` | Verified |

### B2: Gateway to PostgreSQL

| ID | STRIDE | Threat | Mitigation | Evidence | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

A restored copy is read as the runtime role as well: `scripts/verify_restore.py --check-fence` fails a restore whose runtime role `check_runtime` refuses, or under which the fence of any tenant the copy holds, named or not, shows it another tenant's rows or hides its own history ([RECOVERY.md](RECOVERY.md#the-verifier)). | S, CC8; `tests/test_execution_database.py::test_all_execution_tables_isolate_colliding_tenant_objects`, `tests/test_fenced_reads.py`, `tests/test_recovery.py::test_a_policy_that_lets_one_tenant_read_another_is_reported_as_crossing`, `::test_the_fence_check_reads_every_tenant_the_copy_holds_not_only_the_named_ones`, `::test_a_runtime_url_that_bypasses_row_security_is_unsafe_and_crosses` | Verified, see R1 |
| T2.2 | T | Runtime role rewrites authority data (mappings, credentials, inventory, facts) | Owner-owned tables and restricted grants; `resource_facts` is SELECT-only for the runtime. | CC8, CC9; `tests/test_resource_facts_schema.py` | Verified |
| T2.3 | T | Budget, approval or target-claim races | One global lock order, tenant control row then audit head (`execution/store.py`); concurrency proven on real PostgreSQL. | D, L, V; `tests/test_execution.py::test_concurrent_principals_cannot_exceed_shared_first_capacity` | Verified |
| T2.4 | T | Audit history truncated or altered | Per-tenant hash chain with a separately retained trusted head (`audit.py`); export verification detects tampering relative to that head. `audit-verify`, which the jobs service runs hourly, reads every workspace's chain on the live database again and places each retained checkpoint on it, and a chain that fails is logged as `audit.chain_failed` and fails the run (`audit_verify.py`, AV1 to AV7). | `tests/test_replay_verify.py`, `tests/test_audit.py`, `tests/test_audit_verify.py` | Verified, see R2 |
| T2.5 | D | Long transactions or lock waits starve admission | Statement timeout of 10 seconds and lock timeout of 5 seconds per transaction. Measured: about 40 denied admissions per second per tenant with latency growing linearly with concurrency; the runtime's concurrency cap now equals the pool capacity so callers queue on the runtime semaphore instead of timing out on the pool. | `scripts/admission_benchmark.py`; [OPERATIONS.md](OPERATIONS.md#health-and-capacity) | Accepted |
| T2.6 | I | Secrets at rest in the gateway database | Tokens, sessions, CSRF values, recovery codes and report secrets are stored as digests. One usable secret is stored in plaintext: the Arcade correlation row's report secret while its grant is live (R17). Provider dispatch tokens, evidence keys and the Graph client secret never enter the database ([CREDENTIALS.md](CREDENTIALS.md)). | `tests/test_mfa_sessions.py::test_enrollment_seed_is_encrypted_and_expires`, `tests/test_directory_store.py::test_a_registration_is_a_draft_whose_secret_is_only_ever_ciphertext`, `::test_a_linked_account_signs_in_and_the_refresh_token_is_only_ciphertext` | Partial, see R17 |
| T2.7 | T | A limit scope is satisfied or violated by mutable state: a membership, account or inventory change rewrites what retained impact means, or history recorded before the scope existed counts as room in its budget | Every scope counts a filter over retained `execution_impact` rows whose `account_id`, `group_ids` and `facts` were written once at reservation from the frozen plan and the memberships read inside the fence; counting never joins current membership or inventory, a row lacking an attribute never counts toward the scope that needs it, a group, account or selector budget is one counter for every agent, run and adapter under the same tenant fence, and membership changes take that fence (`execution/store.py`, `administration.py`). Replay reconstructs every scope from captures and reports unknown membership as `UNKNOWN_GROUP_MEMBERSHIP`, never as room. | SL1, SL2, SL3, SL5, SL6, SL8, SL9, SL12; `tests/test_limit_scopes.py`, `tests/test_limit_scopes_schema.py::test_backfill_derives_account_and_facts_from_frozen_plans`, `tests/test_replay_scenarios.py::test_sl9_unknown_membership_of_a_retained_unit_is_a_lower_bound_not_room` | Verified |
| T2.8 | E | The runtime writes a signup request, a slot's lifecycle or an invitation directly, or forges a delivery the outbox never queued | Every consequential write on `signup_requests`, `onboarding_slots` and `onboarding_invitations` happens inside a fenced `SECURITY DEFINER` function with no actor (`onboarding_invite`, `onboarding_invitation_rekey`, `sandbox_expire`, `sandbox_notice`, `signup_request`, `signup_allocate`, `signup_mark`), owned by the schema owner and executable by the runtime; the runtime holds `SELECT` and nothing else on the three, and on the outbox holds `UPDATE` on exactly seven delivery columns and no `INSERT` or `DELETE`, so a message names the request, class, slot and invitation the function wrote and nothing can re-point one. `check_runtime` pins the function set and the table set, and since `0065_dormant_grants` holds these grants, the seven columns included, to exactly what the hosted plugin claims, which is nothing on a database whose processes load no plugin (T2.11); both new tables are forced row-level security under the tenant policy every table has (`migrations/versions/0039_signups.py`, `db.py`, and the hosted plugin's own code). | hosted: OS1, OS9b, OS9c, OS9d, OS9e | Verified |
| T2.9 | I | A store function reads without the space predicate and returns another space's rows | **Forced row-level security does not catch this.** A function that forgets its tenant predicate returns nothing because the database refuses it; one that forgets its **space** predicate returns another customer's rows, and there is no second line. A person's read computes the visible set with one function (`spaces.visible_spaces`), inside its own transaction, and its predicate on that set comes from one helper (`spaces.space_clause`), appended to the statement or substituted at the statement's `/*space*/` marker; a row already read is held to the same set by `Visibility.allows`, and the Spaces list, whose rows are the spaces themselves, binds the set directly. Two narrower rules sit beside that predicate and never replace it. The review scope is written out by hand in each statement that applies it (`board`, `dashboard`, `console_api.search`, `human.records` and `request_context.attach_context`): the approval queue, and the reads of an approver `access_control.review_scoped` names, show only the executions an active assignment in the execution's own space names and, outside the queue, the rows of the spaces a role grant gives that approver broad reads in (`:read_spaces`). Each is joined to the helper's predicate by `AND`, so it can narrow what the visible set shows and never widen it. What an administrator narrowed to spaces may change, rather than read, is a second helper, `spaces.administered_clause`, the rule the definer functions apply since `0072_space_principal_scope` (R25). An isolation test is the control. It reads every table `0041_spaces.py` gave a space through that predicate, parametrized over the migration's own list, and it reads the catalog for every table the schema gives a space, by a `space_id` column or a foreign key into `spaces`: each must be in that list, name the negative tests that plant a row in another space and read it through that table's own readers, or say why its space is not a row a customer owns, so a table given a space later fails the test until it has a case. The readers of a role granted in another space and of a review rule in another space are among those cases. The test is the control, which is why it is named here and why R25 records the residual. | SP1, SP2; `tests/test_spaces_isolation.py::test_every_space_carrying_table_has_a_case`, `::test_a_role_granted_in_another_space_reaches_nothing_in_this_one`, `::test_a_review_rule_in_another_space_routes_nothing_in_this_one` | Verified, see R25 |
| T2.10 | E | Forced row-level security is asked to do a space's work, and a policy comes to depend on a session setting | Every `tenant_isolation` policy stays tenant-keyed in its once-per-statement subquery form, forced, on the three new tables as on every other, and a test asserts that no policy in the schema mentions a space setting at all. The space is a column and a `WHERE` clause and never a second fence (`migrations/versions/0041_spaces.py`). | SP10; `tests/test_spaces_schema.py` | Verified |
| T2.11 | E | A runtime with no plugin reads a table or calls a definer function that only a plugin uses (for example `community_provision`, which creates a workspace with its first administrator) | Every migration ships in the one chain, so these objects exist on every install. `0065_dormant_grants` revoked every privilege the owner had granted PUBLIC, the runtime role and Supabase's `anon`, `authenticated` and `service_role` on the seven dormant tables and seventeen dormant functions of `db.DORMANT`, by the revoke convention of `0063_personal_access_tokens`; a grant by another grantor survives, and `check_dormant` refuses it. An object of the kind added later must be created with no grant but the owner's, which for a function means revoking PUBLIC's default `EXECUTE` (the revoke convention); if PUBLIC or the runtime role keeps a grant, `check_runtime` refuses the database wherever no loaded plugin claims the object; a platform role's grant it cannot see, and on the plugin's own database the plugin's upgrade command revokes either. `check_runtime` requires every dormant object to hold exactly what the process's loaded plugins claim and nothing where none claims it, reading the grants in its existing functions query and comparing them after the revision check, so a grant made by hand refuses readiness (503) and every request that reaches the check; `DELETE` and `TRUNCATE` can never be claimed and stay refused on every table. `check_dormant`, the owner-side check the plugin's upgrade command runs before it commits, also reads the ACLs themselves, because what the runtime role can do folds PUBLIC's grants into its own and cannot see a platform role's: it refuses a grant to PUBLIC or to any role but the runtime's (the owner's own entry apart), one made by any grantor but the owner, one carrying a grant option, and one on another function of a dormant name. No source under `src` outside `db.py` and a plugin's own package names a dormant object as `execbound.<name>` (DC19), the form core's queries use, so hosted tests running with the plugin's grants cannot hide a core path that reads one. One residual: core's `account_change` (0052), which the runtime executes, still writes the dormant `account_preferences` through its `preferences` branch, so a runtime with no plugin can write its own consent row, which nothing reads; that is accepted, and a dormant definer would close it (`db.py`, `migrations/versions/0065_dormant_grants.py`, and the hosted plugin's own code). | DC3, DC4, DC5, DC8, DC19, DC21; hosted: DC11 to DC14; `tests/test_dormant_catalog.py` | Verified |
| T2.12 | E | A plugin's claims, compared on the execution path, weaken the runtime's check: a claim widens the runtime's reach into a core table or function, lets a request through, or runs plugin code where requests are decided | The one exception to the rule that nothing on the execution path calls a plugin: `check_runtime`, which runs in authentication, resolution and the admission fence, compares the database with the claims the loader recorded. The claims are data recorded at each load, after every named plugin has loaded and passed its checks, and validated against core's own catalog: exact types, a dormant name, at least one privilege, each in the grammar (`SELECT`, `INSERT` or `UPDATE` on a table, whole or by column, `EXECUTE` on a function), and never a whole-table and a column claim for one privilege, each refused as `INVALID_CLAIM`. They name only dormant objects, which no core source outside `db.py` names as `execbound.<name>` (DC19; core's `account_change` still writes `account_preferences`, T2.11), so a claim cannot reach a core object. A claim can only refuse: one the database does not match refuses the request, and one it matches changes nothing a request can reach. A later load can add a claim but never change one (`PLUGIN_CONFLICT`), a refused load records none, and no plugin code runs in the check (`db.py`, `plugins.py`). | DC2, DC5, DC6; `tests/test_plugins.py`, `tests/test_dormant_catalog.py` | Verified |
| T2.13 | E, I | The runtime forges, duplicates, re-addresses or re-dates a dormancy warning, or reads another workspace's warnings (hosted service only) | A warning is a row of `dormancy_warnings` in the warned workspace's own tenant, inserted only by `dormancy_warn`, a fenced definer function with no actor that decides inside the fence whether the workspace is due (by `agents_sleep`'s own measure of silence) and who is a live administrator, so the runtime never chooses a recipient, a date or whether to warn. The runtime holds `SELECT` and `UPDATE` on the seven outbox columns and nothing else: no `INSERT`, no `DELETE`, and no write to the recipient, the anchor or the sleep moment. One warning per administrator per approach is a unique key on `(tenant_id, principal_id, silent_since)` with `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`, so overlapping passes add nothing. The table is forced row-level security under the tenant fence; the two cross-tenant functions read through an `owner_drain` policy granted only to the role that owns them and return tenant and warning ids or one count, never an address, a name or a time. All four objects are dormant until the hosted plugin claims them (T2.11) (`migrations/versions/0092_dormancy_warning.py`, and the hosted plugin's own code). | hosted: DW3, DW8, DW9, DW10 | Verified |

### B3: Gateway to protected provider

| ID | STRIDE | Threat | Mitigation | Evidence | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| T3.1 | S | Agent-chosen destination, server-side request forgery or passthrough | Fixed configured origin per account; HTTPX transport with no retries, no redirects, no environment proxies and a bounded timeout (`provider/connector.py`). | F; `tests/test_provider_connector.py`, `tests/test_repository_rules.py` | Verified |
| T3.2 | S | Forged or replayed terminal evidence | Command-bound HMAC journals with key identifiers, verified in constant time; another operation's proof cannot settle this one. | I, P, W; `tests/test_identity_reconciliation.py::test_later_signed_operation_cannot_settle_or_refund_original_effect` | Verified, see R3 |
| T3.3 | T | Target changed between read and mutation | On a binding declared `ENFORCED_BY_PROVIDER`, the provider checks the frozen expected version atomically at mutation; both mock providers do. The Microsoft Graph binding cannot, and T3.15 states what it does instead. | O, U; `tests/test_protected_path.py::test_provider_version_changes_after_gateway_commit_fail_atomically` | Verified |
| T3.4 | T | Retry or refund after an uncertain outcome | A lost response retains `INDETERMINATE` with capacity and target guard held; no resend; a cancellation tombstone fences a late arrival. | I, P, W; `tests/test_protected_path.py::test_missing_journal_does_not_refund_and_cancel_fences_delayed_dispatch` | Verified |
| T3.5 | E | Agent reads provider dispatch or control credentials or evidence keys | Providers run as separate authenticated processes; configuration is private files; startup rejects credentials or keys shared across provider families. | K; `tests/test_surface_configuration.py` | Verified, see R4 |
| T3.6 | D | Slow or hung provider | One send attempt with a bounded timeout: 5 seconds by default and at most 30 for a mock provider's connection, and 10 seconds for Microsoft Graph, each applied to every phase of the exchange rather than as one deadline for the whole of it. Uncertainty is retained rather than retried. | I | Verified |
| T3.7 | R | Evidence key loss or rotation | Keyed rings rotate from the private configuration without a restart, atomically for continuations, form state and every connector under the startup independence rules; a removed key refuses its proofs and leaves the operation uncertain until the key is restored, and `rotation-readiness` shows what still depends on a key (`surfaces/configuration.py`, `rotation.py`). | `tests/test_key_rotation.py` (KR1 to KR8) | Verified, see R9 |
| T3.8 | R | Unsettled operations hold claims and budgets until a person notices | `execbound recover` settles them on a schedule through the same fence, audited request, journal lookup and trusted verifier as manual reconciliation; it never resends, never cancels an attempted operation, requests a tombstone only for never-attempted operations when explicitly enabled, and runs with a single-connection pool (`recovery_run.py`). The scheduled pass, `recover --all-tenants`, holds no workspace credential: it visits every active workspace in the directory of tenants, a workspace made by signup included, and acts in each as that workspace's scheduler principal, with the scheduled check where an administrator's run has the ADMIN recheck (T3.12). `--cancel-unattempted` needs an administrator's mode (`--tenant`, or `--tenants-file`, each as a current ADMIN): the CLI refuses it beside `--all-tenants`, and `recover` and `reconcile_operation` refuse it for a scheduled actor before any provider call (`cli.py`, `provider/gateway.py`). | SJ2, SJ4, SJ7; `tests/test_recovery_run.py` (RC1 to RC13), `tests/test_scheduled_recovery.py` | Verified |
| T3.9 | S | A leaked sample key lets its holder call the endpoint mock as any workspace's sample account | The mock holds synthetic targets only and a sample connection reaches only the configured mock origin; no real connection, credential or configuration is derived from the key. The holder acts on nothing else: the mock refuses a sample token for any tenant it already holds a configured account for, whatever account id the presented command names, because `resources` is keyed by tenant and target and dispatch joins any representation in the tenant; and `samples_install` refuses a workspace that already holds an active connector account, so a sample account can exist only in a workspace with none. The gateway applies the mock's rule too: in a tenant its configuration holds a connector for, it derives no sample connection and offers no samples, so a sample request there is refused at admission rather than dispatched to a 401. The key must be independent of every connector evidence key, continuation key and checkpoint journal key, checked at start and on every rotation, and a changed key is a restart (`provider/sample_accounts.py`, `provider/mock_app.py`, `runtime.py`). | SM3, SM4; `tests/test_sample_accounts.py`, `tests/test_sample_mock.py::test_sm4_a_sample_token_for_any_account_in_a_configured_tenant_answers_401`, `tests/test_sample_runtime.py::test_sm_runtime_a_rotation_may_not_take_up_the_sample_key`, `tests/test_sample_runtime.py::test_sm_runtime_a_tenant_holding_a_configured_connector_gets_no_sample_connection` | Accepted, see R28 |
| T3.10 | S | A forged sample account: an account marked `sample` by anything but the install function, or a derived connection for an account nobody installed | Only the `samples_install` definer function sets `connector_accounts.sample`, for a live administrator of the tenant under its fence; the runtime role writes no registry table and no request field, header or credential attribute reaches the flag; the gateway derives a connection only for an active account marked `sample` whose provider has a `sample_providers` entry, and a configured connector resolves exactly as before. | SM1, SM2, SM8; `tests/test_samples_schema.py::test_sm8_revision_catalog_and_privileges`, `tests/test_sample_runtime.py::test_sm_runtime_an_account_not_marked_sample_never_gets_a_derived_connection` | Verified |
| T3.11 | S | A sample token used across workspaces or accounts | The derivation binds the tenant and the account under a versioned label, so a value derived for one workspace or account fails for any other; the mock derives the expected token from the tenant and account of the command it parses, compares in constant time and answers 401 on any mismatch; admission resolves only accounts of the request's own tenant. | SM3, SM4; `tests/test_sample_accounts.py::test_sm3_a_token_for_one_tenant_or_account_fails_for_another`, `tests/test_sample_mock.py::test_sm4_a_mismatched_tenant_account_key_or_role_answers_401` | Verified |
| T3.12 | E | Scheduled recovery acts in a workspace no administrator authorized | The pass may do only a closed list of things, each a transition the product already makes that leaves the record more conservative or applies verified provider evidence: mark an unreported grant `INDETERMINATE`, settle an operation through the trusted verifier bound to its frozen command or mark an inconclusive journal `INDETERMINATE`, release an asserted claim past the act window, expire a lapsed approval, retire thirty-day-old assistant text, and settle without sending a queued notification whose approval is closed or whose destination is gone. It never dispatches, resends, decides or extends an approval, or requests a tombstone: cancellation needs an administrator's `Identity`, which `recover` requires before its first selection and `reconcile_operation` before any provider call. Only the call chain `tests/test_scheduled_actor.py::test_sj9_only_the_pinned_functions_accept_a_scheduled_actor` pins may accept a `ScheduledActor`, and `store.identity_check` refuses one anywhere else. Every selection and every write the pass makes in a workspace runs `scheduled_check` in the same transaction, which requires the workspace to be active and to be the one the transaction is fenced to, and, once the actor names one, the workspace's own active scheduler principal; a deactivated workspace stops the workspace's run at its next check. The fence, lock order and trusted verifier are the ones an administrator's run uses (`execution/scheduled.py`, `recovery_run.py`, `provider/gateway.py`). | SJ2, SJ6, SJ7, SJ9; `tests/test_scheduled_recovery.py::test_sj7_a_scheduled_actor_never_asks_for_a_tombstone`, `::test_sj7_the_scheduled_run_refuses_cancellation_before_reading_anything`, `tests/test_scheduled_actor.py::test_identity_check_refuses_a_scheduled_actor`, `::test_the_scheduled_check_is_bound_to_the_transactions_own_fence` | Verified |
| T3.13 | S, E | A connector vouches for its own evidence: it returns a forged journal or record together with a verifier, or a reported configuration, that accepts it; or it reads more of the dispatch than it needs | A connector receives the frozen `ProviderCommand` and nothing else of the dispatch, and carries no verifier. The runtime reads each connector's configuration once, validates that copy, rotates that copy, and builds the verifier the gateway settles with from it (`provider/verifiers.py`, `Runtime.evidence_verifier`), matching on the exact configuration type; the verifier it hands out reads the account's current ring each time it is used. Every server caller passes it, which a static test holds, and neither connector package can name `TerminalEvidence`, the evidence the kernel settles on. A connector's own `check_journal` or `check_connector_record` only catches its mistakes early; it decides nothing. | PK5 to PK9; `tests/test_protected_path.py::test_the_verifier_comes_from_the_server_not_the_connector`, `::test_reconciliation_settles_with_the_server_verifier_too`, `::test_the_runtime_verifies_with_the_config_it_was_given_not_what_a_connector_reports`, `tests/test_connector_packages.py::test_every_server_caller_settles_with_the_runtimes_verifier` | Verified |
| T3.14 | I, E | The Microsoft Graph client secret, which can disable real users in a customer's directory, leaks or is sent anywhere but Microsoft | The secret is read from the private configuration's `graph_connectors` block alone, and never travels in a request an agent makes, a failure reason, an error or a representation. The Graph and sign-in origins are pinned in source (`GRAPH_ORIGIN`, `LOGIN_ORIGIN`), so neither configuration nor agent input can move where the secret goes, and the client follows no redirect and trusts no proxy or certificate setting from the environment. The connector performs one operation, `identity.disable`, and refuses a dispatch for another account before any vendor call. What the Entra app registration itself may do is the operator's to scope, so the execute registration should hold only `User.EnableDisableAccount.All` and `User.Read.All` and no directory role ([PROVIDER.md](PROVIDER.md)); keeping the configuration file itself is the operator's too ([CREDENTIALS.md](CREDENTIALS.md)). | `tests/test_graph_connector.py::test_no_failure_puts_the_secret_or_the_token_in_its_reason`, `::test_the_credential_keeps_its_secret_out_of_its_repr`, `::test_the_client_never_follows_a_redirect_or_trusts_the_environment`, `tests/test_graph_dispatch.py::test_a_dispatch_for_another_account_is_refused_before_any_vendor_call` | Partial, see R12 |
| T3.15 | T | The target changes between the Graph connector's read and its write | Graph offers no conditional write, so the binding is declared `CHECKED_BY_CONNECTOR` and the deployment refuses any other class for it. The connector reads the user, compares the composite with the frozen version, refuses before any write on a mismatch, and writes at most once. It signs a record of the frozen and observed composites and the measured window between read and write, which settlement retains. A change inside that window is undetectable, and so is a stale read Graph serves, since its reads are not read-your-writes consistent (PROVIDER.md); the window is that limit, measured, and never described as atomicity. | `tests/test_graph_connector.py::test_a_disable_reads_compares_and_writes_exactly_once`, `::test_a_change_after_the_read_is_undetectable_and_that_is_the_whole_window`, `::test_a_mismatch_refuses_before_any_write_is_attempted`, `tests/test_graph_dispatch.py::test_an_allowed_disable_writes_once_and_retains_the_window_it_measured`, `::test_a_subject_that_moved_is_refused_and_the_record_keeps_the_drift` | Accepted |
| T3.16 | R | A lost Graph response or a refused write is settled from a re-read that cannot tell this connector's write from someone else's | Graph publishes no journal, so the connector offers no lookup and no cancellation, and such an execution stays uncertain rather than settling on a guess. A refused write is retained as uncertain rather than claimed as a failure, and a cancellation cannot refund a Graph operation. The record's signature is ExecBound's own and is never evidence that Microsoft acted, which replay states. | `tests/test_graph_dispatch.py::test_the_connector_offers_no_lookup_and_no_cancellation`, `::test_a_refused_write_is_uncertain_rather_than_a_claimed_failure`, `::test_a_cancellation_cannot_refund_a_graph_operation`, `::test_replay_reads_the_connector_envelope_and_the_window_it_carries` | Verified |

**Transport decision (September 16, 2026).** A provider origin is HTTPS with a certificate the gateway verifies unless it is a loopback IP address, and there is no opt-in for cleartext on a private network. Two alternatives were considered for running the mock providers on a hosting platform whose private network is cleartext: a per-connector opt-in admitting private addresses, rejected because it adds a switch that weakens this boundary and would have to be proven unreachable from every agent input; and TLS served by the mocks themselves through a mounted CA, rejected as certificates for no benefit. The accepted posture is the one the hosted service uses: the mocks are public HTTPS web services that authenticate every call with per-account bearer tokens and sign their evidence, which is the posture of the vendor APIs they stand in for, so the hosted service exercises the connector rule rather than a relaxed one. Internet reachability adds no authority: a mock answers 401 without its token and mutates nothing without the dispatch token; a leaked token is rotated like any credential; a flood against a public mock is T3.6 and leaves operations uncertain rather than wrong.

### B4: Human browser to human surface

| ID | STRIDE | Threat | Mitigation | Evidence | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| T4.1 | S | Session forgery or theft | Opaque hashed sessions that end after 15 minutes idle and 8 hours at most (`SESSION_LIFETIME`, `SESSION_ABSOLUTE`); HttpOnly and SameSite=strict cookies with the `__Host-` prefix when served over HTTPS; expiry rechecked inside decision transactions (`surfaces/sessions.py`). | X; `tests/test_human_surface.py`, `tests/test_human_sessions.py` | Verified |
| T4.2 | T | Cross-site or forged form submission | Exact Origin check, CSRF token and an HMAC-signed continuation binding credential, tenant, execution, plan, expiry and nonce (`surfaces/tokens.py`). | X; `tests/test_human_surface.py::test_forged_human_forms_do_not_approve` | Verified |
| T4.3 | E | Approval outside the assigned scope, by an ADMIN, or against a changed plan | Server-side scope and role checks before and after lock waits; the continuation binds the plan hash; a changed plan invalidates the approval. | G, K, U; `tests/test_execution.py::test_pending_authority_changes_invalidate_approval` | Verified |
| T4.4 | I | Agent or provider strings executed in the browser | The console renders every string as text through React and never through a DOM sink: `dangerouslySetInnerHTML`, `innerHTML` and its relatives, `eval`, `new Function` and string timers are lint errors with a fixture apiece, and its policy is `default-src 'none'; script-src 'self'; style-src 'self'; img-src 'self' data:; font-src 'self'; connect-src 'self'` with `require-trusted-types-for 'script'` and no policy registered, so a raw string into a sink is refused by the browser itself. The pages that stay server-rendered keep Jinja autoescape with strict undefined variables and the pages' own policy, which allows only the packaged stylesheet, the packaged fonts and the showcase's one refresh module. Every response carries `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `X-Frame-Options: DENY` and HSTS when secure (`app.py`, `surfaces/console.py`); reviewer comments are bounded, printable and refused when credential-shaped. | X, CC12, CF10, HV10, HV16; `tests/test_browser_surfaces.py`, `tests/test_browser_console.py`, `tests/test_browser_console_shell.py`, `tests/test_approval_detail.py`, `tests/test_browser_approval_detail.py` | Verified |
| T4.5 | D | Login brute force | Sign-in attempts are limited per credential selector before any lookup and per direct peer address; refusals carry `Retry-After`. The password form and a provider's callback share that limiter under a key that is the SHA-256 of the normalized address, so no address is held in the limiter's memory in the clear and neither way in walks around the other's bound (`surfaces/sign_in_pages.py`). | `tests/test_rate_limits.py::test_login_attempts_beyond_the_limit_are_refused_even_with_the_right_secret`, `tests/test_sign_in_surface.py::test_attempts_beyond_the_limit_are_refused_even_with_the_right_password` | Verified |
| T4.6 | D | One unreadable row fails the whole tenant listing | A frozen plan naming a contract absent from the runtime catalog renders an escaped label instead of a 503. | `tests/test_human_surface.py::test_listing_renders_unknown_contract_label_instead_of_failing`, `::test_listing_renders_unknown_action_label_instead_of_failing` | Verified |
| T4.7 | E | A single approver suffices for a high-risk action | Bundle `quorum` rules require two to five distinct human approvers for an obligation, frozen on the approval at creation; distinctness is the `approval_decisions` primary key, one denial ends the approval, partial approvals expire with the window, and consumption verifies the exact count and every approver's current credential and scope inside the fence (`execution/store.py`). A quorum of one is unchanged behavior. | `tests/test_approval_quorum.py` (QA1 to QA10, QA12) | Verified |
| T4.9 | I | A notification leaks a secret or an untrusted string, or its link decides an approval | Payloads carry identifiers, catalog labels, timestamps and the fixed approval page link only; an email's subject and body carry labels reduced to letters, digits, spaces and `._:/()-`, so no header, markup or link syntax survives; a rendered message also names its workspace and space, both reduced the same way, and the requesting agent by its tenant-local handle ("Agent 3", built from the principal's counting number), never by the label an administrator gave it or any string the agent supplied, while the webhook body stays byte for byte as documented; a reminder (`approval.reminder`) is the same message again with its own subject, first line and header, and a webhook body that differs from the announcement's only in `kind`; an outcome message (`approval.closed`) follows the same rules and says less, the outcome, its time, the workspace and the link, with no target fact, agent or person and a fixed sentence that it decides nothing and does not say whether the action ran, and its webhook body is its own documented kind; destinations, recipient addresses and the relay come solely from the operator's private file, webhooks are HMAC-signed and email travels only over verified TLS; Slack and Teams URLs are secrets pinned to the vendors' hosts, never logged or rendered, and their blocks and cards are `plain_text` with the same reduced labels;
