| T4.18 | D | A stranger mines the public signup page: enumerating which addresses already hold a workspace, draining capacity, or flooding the queue from behind the proxy every visitor shares | Every submission answers one page with one status, for an admission, a place on the waitlist and each of the eight refusals alike, so nothing on the page distinguishes them; refusals reach the structured log and the operator's own list, never the browser. The controls are counts taken inside `signup_request` against the snapshot the row is written in, not anything the browser is asked to prove: a per-address cooldown, a per-domain cap with free-mail domains held to their own lower daily cap, an hourly and a daily cap overall, refusal of a disposable domain, and a honeypot field recorded as a refusal. **Since 23 September 2026, `open` mode's hard ceiling is `signups.capacity` (the count of `PROVISIONED` requests in the operator tenant), not a seeded slot pool**: a submission is admitted against it here and the count is retaken, authoritatively, under the operator's fence when the link is completed (T4.34 below); the pool remains the ceiling only for operator-minted invitations and for `invitation` messages a request already queued before that release. The page itself is CSRF-cookied like `/login`, refuses a query string, a bearer header, a wrong `Origin` or an extra field before the database, and keeps the per-peer window, which behind a proxy is a flood guard on the process rather than a limit on any one caller (the hosted plugin's own code and pages). **Since 2 October 2026, a Google or GitHub attempt at `/signup/{provider}` passes through the identical counts**, under the identical fence, as an email submission; once the provider has proven the address, every page that follows is one of the emailed link's own pages (the generic page, the waitlist page, the naming form), so a provider flow opens no surface a stranger could mine differently from the one this row already describes. | hosted: OS5, OS5b, OS5c, OS5d, OS14c; `tests/test_browser_accessibility.py` | Verified |
| T4.19 | I | A prospect's email address escapes the operator's own list into an audit event, a log line, a page or a report | The address is written by a fenced function into `signup_requests` in the operator tenant and read back only over the owner connection, by the operator's own tooling. Structured logs carry the request id, the domain and whether it is free mail, never the address; `tenant.claimed`, `tenant.expired`, `tenant.extended` and `tenant.converted` carry no address; the signup and confirmation pages render no submitted value at all; the operator's reports carry only counts, domains and durations. Retention nulls the address thirty days after a refusal or an expiry and ninety after a claim, leaving the domain and the timestamps, and the message a prospect receives is addressed from the row rather than from anything the outbox holds (`logging_schema.py`, and the hosted plugin's own code). | hosted: OS8, OS10, OS13 | Verified |
| T4.20 | S, D | Password guessing, credential stuffing or account enumeration at `/login` | A password is never stored: only a salted `hashlib.scrypt` hash at n=2^15, r=8, p=1, with the algorithm and all four parameters in their own columns beside it, so a row written under older parameters keeps verifying and raising them is a write. Attempts are refused per peer address and per address digest before any lookup. A wrong address and a wrong password answer 401 with one sentence, and cost the same: when the address belongs to nobody the hash runs anyway against a decoy record built at import under the current parameters. The private reason (`UNKNOWN`, `MISMATCH`, `INACTIVE`) reaches `record_failure` and the structured log alone, attributed to the person where there is one. Verification runs on a worker thread under a concurrency bound, so a flood queues rather than taking the process (`passwords.py`, `surfaces/sign_in_pages.py`). | `tests/test_passwords.py`, `tests/test_sign_in_store.py`, `tests/test_sign_in_surface.py`, `tests/test_browser_sign_in.py::test_a_wrong_password_and_an_unknown_address_say_the_same_thing` | Verified |
| T4.21 | S | A forged, replayed or misdirected provider assertion signs the wrong person in | Each provider's endpoints are pinned in its own module and only the client id, the client secret and the redirect URI are configured; the redirect URI must equal this deployment's own origin plus that provider's callback path or the server refuses to start. Every flow carries PKCE S256, a random `state` and a random `nonce` in one `__Host-`, HttpOnly, SameSite=Lax cookie deleted on first use, so a replayed callback has nothing to match and a `state` mismatch is refused. Google's ID token is verified against Google's JWKS with `alg` settled before any key is fetched, one refetch on an unknown `kid` and then a refusal, and `iss`, `aud`, `azp`, `exp`, `iat` and `nonce` all checked; `email_verified` must be true. Microsoft's ID token is verified the same way against Microsoft's own pinned key set, with the issuer taken from the token's own tenant, the personal-account tenant refused, `xms_edov` required and the subject the tenant and object identifiers together (T4.85). GitHub has no assertion to verify, so the account comes from two authenticated calls and the only address that may find a person is the one GitHub marks both `primary` and `verified`; the access token is `user:email` only and is discarded after the lookup. **A flow started at `/login` still refuses an account matching no person and creates nothing there.** A flow started at `/signup` is different: once a loaded plugin's signup point has checked that the subject identifies nobody and the address holds no account, it may record a request and create a **new** account for it, exactly as the emailed link already does, and never by attaching to an existing one. The subject is bound once and identifies the person afterwards, and a subject already bound to somebody else is refused rather than moved, on either path (`oidc.py`, `github.py`, `microsoft.py`, `signin.py`, migrations `0040_sign_in` and `0091_microsoft_sign_in`). | `tests/test_oidc.py`, `tests/test_github_sign_in.py`, `tests/test_microsoft_sign_in.py`, `tests/test_sign_in_surface.py`, `tests/test_sign_in_store.py::test_a_subject_bound_to_one_person_is_never_moved_to_another`; hosted: MI7 | Verified |
| T4.22 | E, I | A set-password code reaches the wrong person, survives its use, or leaves the previous holder signed in | A code is `ebp1.<tenant>.<reset>.<secret>`, stored only as its SHA-256, single use, expiring between an hour and fourteen days, revoked by five wrong secrets and by the minting of the next one, and never present in a URL: what is handed over is the address to open and the code to type. An expired, redeemed, revoked, superseded and unknown code all fail identically. Redeeming revokes every live credential and unrevoked session of that person in the same statement sequence and returns their identifiers so the caller audits each one in the same transaction. The `ebp1.` prefix is part of the shared secret definition, so a rendered page or an export carrying a code fails the guard (`migrations/versions/0040_sign_in.py`, `passwords.py`, `secrets.py`). | `tests/test_sign_in_store.py`, `tests/test_secrets.py`, `tests/test_browser_sign_in.py::test_a_code_works_once_and_the_second_person_to_try_it_is_refused` | Verified |
| T4.23 | I | Resolving an address before a tenant is known reads more than that one address | The login form takes an address and no tenant, so the read happens outside the fence — through two **single-row** RLS policies rather than a function with table-wide authority. A `SELECT` on `principals` is permitted only for the row whose own email equals `execbound.sign_in_email`, and on `principal_identities` only for the row whose own provider and subject equal `execbound.sign_in_subject`. Setting a marker therefore answers about the address or the account the caller already typed and about nothing else: no listing, no enumeration, no other tenant's row. Everything after the resolution is an ordinary fenced read (`migrations/versions/0040_sign_in.py`, `passwords.py`). | `tests/test_sign_in_store.py::test_the_marker_reveals_one_row_and_never_a_listing`, `tests/test_sign_in_store.py::test_the_subject_marker_reveals_one_row_and_never_a_listing` | Verified |
| T4.24 | T | A form submission that starts a provider flow is forged, or the redirect it follows is blocked or pointed elsewhere | Starting a flow is a `POST` with the login CSRF cookie compared with the field and the exact `Origin` check, like every other mutation, rather than a bare link. `form-action` governs the redirect a form submission follows, so the pages' policy names each configured provider's authorization host — pinned in that provider's module, present only because a block named the provider, and absent entirely from a deployment with none. Nothing a request sends adds a host (`surfaces/console.py`, `app.py`). | `tests/test_sign_in_surface.py::test_a_configured_provider_is_named_in_form_action_and_nothing_else_is`, `tests/test_browser_sign_in.py::test_a_configured_provider_signs_a_matched_person_in` | Verified |
| T4.25 | E | A person sets the session's current space to one they do not belong to, or a stale column grants a read after they were removed | `session_set_space` is a `SECURITY DEFINER` function rather than a runtime `UPDATE` grant, and it verifies the space against `space_members` (or tenant administration) in the database, so "a session's current space is one of the person's spaces" is a database invariant. It is belt and braces: every read recomputes the visible set inside the fence and narrows the current space to it, so a person removed between two page loads falls back to "All spaces" at once and a forged or stale column grants nothing (`migrations/versions/0041_spaces.py`, `spaces.py`). | SP4; `tests/test_spaces.py`, `tests/test_console_api_spaces.py` | Verified |
| T4.26 | I | A record in another space is opened by its identifier | Membership is the authority and the current space is only a view: a record in a space the person does not belong to is 404 whatever the session holds, and one in another of their **own** spaces is answered and names its space so the shell can follow it. There is no `?space=` anywhere, so editing a URL cannot ask for another space's rows and a shared link is read in the reader's own space (`spaces.Visibility.allows`, `surfaces/agents_pages.py`, `surfaces/space_pages.py`). | SP1, SP11; `tests/test_console_api_spaces.py` | Verified |
| T4.27 | T | A decision lands while its space is being archived | The archive takes the tenant fence before it calls the definer function, and `decide_approval` rechecks the space inside the kernel's own transaction under that same fence, so the two are totally ordered: whichever takes the fence first commits and the other is refused `SPACE_ARCHIVED`. Exactly one of the two commits, under two connections on real PostgreSQL (`surfaces/space_pages.py`, `execution/approvals.py`). | SP9; `tests/test_spaces_kernel.py` | Verified |
| T4.28 | S, T | A tenant's registered directory returns a forged, replayed or misdirected assertion, or a browser is walked into somebody else's flow | One redirect URI serves every tenant and is checked against this deployment's own origin, so an authorization code can only ever be delivered here. Discovery is fetched once at registration and validated hard — the document's own `issuer` must equal the registered one, and the authorization, token and JWKS endpoints must be https on the issuer's own host — and the three endpoints are then stored on the row, so a document that changes later cannot move a live flow. Every flow carries PKCE S256, a random `state` and a random `nonce` in one `__Host-`, HttpOnly, SameSite=Lax cookie deleted on first use; the cookie also names the provider and either an invitation or a self-linking principal, never both, **and carries a MAC** over all six values. The MAC is what a session cookie cannot be here: the callback is a cross-site redirect back, so a SameSite=Strict session is not sent with it, and a cookie a caller could write would let them name somebody else's principal and bind their own directory account to it. Every key in the ring is tried, so a rotation strands no flow already in the air. The ID token is verified here with `alg` settled before any key is fetched and refused unless `RS256`, `PS256` or `ES256`; each verifies only with a key of its own family, RSA for the first two and P-256 for `ES256`, because a directory's keys are filed by `kid` and family and a header never chooses how a key of another kind is used, and an `ES256` signature is accepted only in its 64-byte JWS form; one refetch on an unknown `kid` and then a refusal, and `iss`, `aud`, `azp`, `exp`, `iat`, `nonce` and `tid` all checked; an Entra issuer must name one directory, so `common` is refused at registration and an assertion from another Microsoft tenant is refused at sign-in. RFC 9207's `iss` is compared when the directory sends it. A subject with no link is refused and creates nothing (`directory.py`, `surfaces/directory_pages.py`, migration `0043_directory_sign_in`). | `tests/test_directory.py::test_every_claim_this_server_checks_has_its_own_refusal`, `tests/test_directory.py::test_an_unknown_key_is_refetched_once_and_then_refused`, `tests/test_directory.py::test_a_family_a_warm_cache_lacks_reads_the_set_once_more_and_is_refused`, `tests/test_directory.py::test_an_algorithm_this_server_cannot_verify_is_refused_before_any_key_is_fetched`, `tests/test_directory.py::test_an_es256_assertion_verifies`, `tests/test_directory.py::test_an_algorithm_finds_no_key_of_another_family_under_its_kid`, `tests/test_directory.py::test_an_es256_signature_in_any_other_form_is_refused`, `tests/test_directory.py::test_an_ec_entry_this_server_must_not_use_is_skipped`, `tests/test_directory.py::test_the_signature_check_never_verifies_es256_with_a_p384_key`, `tests/test_directory_surface.py::test_a_replayed_callback_has_nothing_to_match`, `tests/test_directory_surface.py::test_a_subject_nobody_linked_is_refused_and_creates_nothing` | Verified |

| T4.31 | S | A Community signup verification link is leaked, forwarded, sits in a shared mailbox, or is opened by a mail scanner or link-prefetcher before the person does | The secret lives only in the URL fragment, which `GET /signup/verify` never reads server-side and the page's own script strips from the address bar and history before anything else can act on it; the page itself is static and side-effect free, so any number of opens by a scanner or a prefetcher changes nothing. The script sends the exchange by itself when the fragment is a valid token, so a scanner that runs scripts also posts it; the exchange only reads what the link is for, a correct token is not a miss, and nothing exists until the create form is sent with a password and a workspace name. The exchange is `POST`-only, and the token it posts is checked against `verify_token_hash` inside `signup_verify_check`/`community_provision`, which count a wrong digest toward a five-miss lockout (`verify_failures`) rather than answering differently for a guess and a hit. The link expires thirty minutes after it is minted (`verify_expires_at`, `signup_verify_rekey`) and a resend rotates `verify_generation` and the digest, so a previously leaked link dies the moment a fresh one is sent. Every signup page is `Cache-Control: no-store`, and the site's default `Referrer-Policy: same-origin` means a same-origin navigation carries no cross-origin Referer and the secret is never in a query string to be logged or cached along the way (`migrations/versions/0055_community_signup.py`, and the hosted plugin's own code, pages and script). Closing signups does not cancel a link already queued for delivery: the delivery pass still sends it, and its completion now refuses under `closed` mode (`SIGNUPS_CLOSED`, checked before any write or password hash) rather than provisioning, leaving the request `VERIFYING` with its link untouched until the operator reopens signups or the link's own thirty minutes pass. | hosted: VS3, VS9, VS12, VS18, VS20, VS20b, VS32 | Verified |
| T4.32 | T | Two browsers, or one browser retried after a lost response, complete one verification link and end up with two workspaces or two accounts | `community_provision` takes a row lock (`FOR UPDATE`) on the `signup_requests` row before it reads or writes anything else, inside the operator tenant's own fence, so two concurrent completions of the same link serialize on that lock; the second to run reads `state = 'PROVISIONED'` and returns `ALREADY` with the first's tenant id rather than provisioning again. Two different requests sharing one account are still one account: `user_accounts.email` is unique, and a new account's insert or its email-holder check inside the same transaction turns a unique violation into `ACCOUNT_EXISTS` rather than a second row. The consumed Community allocation is a unique index on `community_allocations` keyed to the account, so a second self-created workspace for the same account fails closed the same way. Nothing here mints a session for a retried completion: an `ALREADY` outcome never carries the new account's version at all, and the hosted plugin's sign-in step refuses to mint a credential without one, so only the one call that actually created the account signs anybody in: a losing racer or a later replay of the create form gets the "Your workspace is ready. Sign in." page instead, and a replay of the signed-in form gets the "Your new workspace is ready." page its first completion showed only when that workspace is in the signed-in account's own workspace menu, the `ready` page otherwise, because `community_provision` answers `ALREADY` on the token before it compares the account. | hosted: VS8, VS10, VS12h, VS12i | Verified |

That route then re-authenticates the session and account cookie inside the provisioning transaction and `community_provision` checks the signed-in account's own email against the request's (`v_account.email <> v_request.email` is `ACCOUNT_MISMATCH`), so a session for a different address than the one the link names is refused rather than silently attached. Capacity itself is counted twice: advisory when a request is submitted, and authoritative (a fresh `count(*) FROM signup_requests WHERE state = 'PROVISIONED'` under the operator tenant's own fence) the instant a link is completed, so two links racing the last unit of capacity cannot both provision (T4.32's lock already serializes it) and a submission that looked admitted can still be waitlisted at completion with no partial workspace left behind. | T4.81 | S, E | Someone who reads, intercepts or guesses a password reset link takes over an account, or uses the reset to skip its second factor (hosted service only) | The hosted service's own code mints a link only for an active account that already holds a password and has a verified address, and mails it only to that address. Its secret is 256 random bits in the URL fragment, which no server receives, and only its SHA-256 is stored. One live link per account: a newer request or a resend replaces it, and each send attempt mints its own, so an uncertain attempt's link is dead before the next. A link lasts 30 minutes from its send, is bound to the address it was mailed to and to the account's version at the send, so an address change or any sign-in change ends it, and five wrong secrets end it; it is spent once under a row lock. Opening it and pressing Continue change nothing. Completion requires a current authenticator step above the last one used, or an unused recovery code that is then removed, whenever a factor is enabled; wrong codes count toward the five-attempt lock every session shares, and a completion that meets or sets that lock ends the link. The reset runs under the account lock and every workspace's fence, moves the account's version, ends every account session and every workspace session (each a `human_session.revoked` with `PASSWORD_RESET`) and revokes every personal access token in every workspace the account belongs to, records the reset in each workspace where it changed something or the account is active and for the account itself, emails a notice naming the time and the support address, and signs nobody in. | hosted: RP1, RP3, RP4, RP5, RP6, RP7, RP9, RP11 | Verified |

Refusals, bot-trap hits and unknown addresses never count toward those caps, and past 2,000 rows an hour only a request that will be mailed still records a row, so a script can neither close the caps for others nor grow the table without bound. The email is sent after the answer by a background task, so the answer's timing does not depend on the outcome; that task runs without an operator identity, so it may lease only the one message the server generated and its own transaction queued for the same request, never one named by the client or one the scheduled retry holds, while the retry keeps the operator identity check. The form is CSRF-cookied, `Origin`-checked and honeypotted like `/signup`, and the relay's daily budget counts signup and reset mail together, with dormancy warnings, which may spend only half of it (T5.41), and defers rather than drops. | hosted: RP1, RP2, RP8, RP9 | Verified |

Every switch needs the account cookie, CSRF and the exact Origin, and the console API's switch also needs the displayed workspace generation. | T4.85 | S | An account in another Microsoft organization asserts an address it does not own, or a personal Microsoft account signs in | The deployment's Microsoft button uses the multi-tenant `organizations` authority, a recorded deviation from the single-tenant rule a workspace's own directory keeps, which still stands for directories. What replaces the pin: the ID token's issuer must equal the issuer of the token's own `tid`, so a token is believed only about the tenant that signed it; the personal-account tenant is refused here as well as by the authority; the key set, the endpoints and `RS256` are pinned in source, `alg` is settled before any key is fetched and the signature is checked before any claim is read; `ver`, `aud`, `azp`, `exp`, `iat`, `nbf` and `nonce` are checked; the subject is `tid:oid`, so an object identifier in another tenant is another person, and `sub` (pairwise per application) and `preferred_username` (unverified) are never read. The address is believed only when `xms_edov` is exactly the JSON value `true`, and then only where any provider's address is: to start a **new** account through a plugin's signup, or to link the provider from a signed-in account whose own verified address equals it. Sign-in is by subject alone, and a workspace that requires its own directory is never opened by it. Nothing turns the button on: a deployment offers it only when its operator adds a `sign_in.microsoft` block. Residual: the administrator of a tenant that verified a domain can assert any address in that domain, which is that domain's owner speaking. Gap: the claim's JSON type is checked against Microsoft's documentation and a stand-in token; a sign-in through a real registration has not yet confirmed it (`microsoft.py`, `oidc.py`, `surfaces/configuration.py`, migration `0091_microsoft_sign_in`). | `tests/test_microsoft_sign_in.py`, `tests/test_sign_in_surface.py::test_mi3_microsoft_signs_in_and_links_by_subject_only`, `tests/test_microsoft_migration.py`; hosted: MI7, MI8, MI9 | Partial |

### B5: Owner tooling, ADMIN and policy

| ID | STRIDE | Threat | Mitigation | Evidence | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| T5.1 | E | An agent or approver creates, edits or activates a bundle | ADMIN-only lifecycle inside the tenant fence, audited; content is immutable and digest-bound (`policy/store.py`). | `tests/test_policy_store.py` | Verified |
| T5.2 | T | Malformed policy, schema mismatch or engine error changes a decision | Structured parsing and validation fail closed; both policy sets are evaluated; any error denies. | N, CC1, CC7; `tests/test_cedar.py`, `tests/test_context_bundle_validation.py` | Verified |
| T5.3 | T | A writer allowlist admits a label the agent can write | Documented misconfiguration; scenario T protection depends on ADMIN naming only trusted writer labels. | CC6; [POLICY.md](POLICY.md) | Accepted, see R5 |
| T5.4 | T | Wrong trusted provisioning (inventory, canonical mapping, facts, incidents) | Outside the agent input boundary. Facts and incidents enter only through the owner-connection importer, which validates every value against the contract catalog, accepts only a writer label the owner-provisioned source allows, refuses observations that go backwards and reused versions, never creates registry rows, and audits each import with the manifest digest (`provisioning.py`); canonical mapping stays owner SQL. | CP1, CP4 to CP9, CP11; `tests/test_context_provisioning.py` | Verified for facts and incidents; Accepted for canonical mapping, see R10 |
| T5.5 | E | A Cedar binding change silently alters decisions | Pinned `cedarpy`; the adapter rejects incomplete diagnostics; a guard test fails with a review message if the private entry points change. | `tests/test_cedar.py::test_pinned_native_entry_points_keep_their_shape` | Partial, see R6 |
| T5.6 | R | Administrative actions without attribution | Every lifecycle change, reconciliation and acknowledgement is audited with current authority rechecked. | `tests/test_audit.py`, replay export tests | Verified |
| T5.7 | E | Agents keep acting during an incident because nothing halts one tenant | A workspace administrator's emergency stop (T5.32) on the tenant control row, read inside the fence by every writer: new admission, approval decisions and dispatch attempts refuse with `TENANT_STOPPED` while settlement continues; activation and release are audited with reasons and replay flags decisions inside a stop period (`execution/stops.py`). | `tests/test_emergency_stop.py` (ES1 to ES12) | Verified |
| T5.8 | E | An administrator weakens a tenant to observe-only, or an observed result is mistaken for authorization | The mode lives on the forced-RLS control row and changes only through a current workspace administrator (T5.32) inside the fence with a required reason, audited as `tenant.mode_changed`; an observed request never receives a ticket, approval, reservation or claim, ends in the terminal `OBSERVED` state whose `observed` object is a record and not authority, never counts toward an enforced budget, and replay reports an observed execution with any effect as `OBSERVED_WITH_EFFECT` (`execution/modes.py`, `execution/admission.py`). Switching to `OBSERVE` is a trusted ADMIN capability accepted for onboarding. | `tests/test_observe_mode.py` (OB1 to OB12) | Verified |
| T5.9 | T | A compromised or faulty adapter feeds false context | The adapter holds no database authority; the importer refuses values outside the catalog domains, sources of another tenant or kind, unknown writers, future and regressing observations and relinked incidents, and withdrawal of evidence (retraction, deactivation) can only deny. A wrong but well-formed value remains the operator's provisioning responsibility. | CP4, CP5, CP6, CP8, CP10 | Partial, see R10 |
| T5.10 | E | A web bug or a captured ADMIN session mints administrators or widens the registry | The gateway holds no table write privilege on principals, credentials or approval scopes; the five `SECURITY DEFINER` functions of migration 0021 read the tenant from the fence setting, verify the acting ADMIN inside the database, refuse ADMIN kinds, principals and credentials, bound lifetimes and clock skew, and cannot touch tenants, accounts, mappings or canonical targets; the pages recheck session and role before and after each call and audit every change with the acting credential; `check_runtime` asserts the exact function catalog, definer mode, ownership and privilege (`db.py`, `surfaces/admin_pages.py`). | CA1 to CA9; `tests/test_admin_pages.py` | Verified |
| T5.11 | E | An agent, approver or the runtime role widens a limit scope by creating or joining a group, or a bundle names a group, account or fact the tenant does not have | Groups and memberships are owner-provisioned authority data like approval scopes: forced RLS, runtime SELECT only, written only by `group-create`, `group-assign` and `group-unassign` over the owner connection under an ADMIN `--actor`, active AGENT principals only, at most 64 memberships each, audited as `group.created`, `group.assigned` and `group.revoked`; the store refuses a rule naming an unknown action, group, inactive account or unserved fact with its field location, at validation and at activation. The console's rules write takes a single-action group, account or space budget only with a qualifier its read offered, all read inside the tenant fence (the tenant's groups, the active accounts the draft's own mappings name, and the spaces the draft may serve that the person may see), and refuses any other at the budget's field without echoing the identifier, so another tenant's group, account or space never reaches a draft through the form; the store's refusal above still stands behind it. **Admission does not re-run those checks**: it reads the active bundle through `execution.store.active_bundle`, which revalidates no component, so a group removed after activation is not refused there. What bounds that is the write path rather than a recheck — no command deletes a group, the runtime holds `SELECT` only on both tables, and the membership foreign key has no cascade, so removal takes the owner connection — and `store.memberships` reads the principal's groups inside the fence at every decision, so a rule's counting follows current provisioning rather than a stale bundle claim (`administration.py`, `policy/store.py`, `execution/store.py`, migration `0022_limit_scopes`). See [POLICY.md](POLICY.md). | SL7, SL10, SL11; `tests/test_admin_cli.py::test_group_lifecycle`, `tests/test_limit_scopes_schema.py::test_runtime_cannot_write_group_tables`, `tests/test_policy_store.py::test_scoped_rules_fail_validation_with_locations`, `tests/test_console_api_rules.py::test_a_qualifier_of_another_tenant_is_refused_at_its_field` | Partial |
| T5.12 | E | A departed or compromised person keeps a credential or a session; a console user deprovisions an administrator or themselves | Deactivation is one definer function under the 0021 actor check that sets the principal inactive and revokes every live credential and unrevoked session in the same statement sequence, and the page appends the events in the same transaction, so there is no state in which the person is inactive but a credential or session still works; the function refuses an ADMIN target and the actor, so deactivation never removes an administrator, and nothing is deleted; making or unmaking an administrator through a role grant is T5.28 (`migrations/versions/0036_people.py`, `surfaces/people_pages.py`). | `tests/test_people_pages.py`, `tests/test_console_api_people.py`, `tests/test_browser_console_directory.py` | Verified |
| T5.13 | S | A wrong or forged link attributes a principal's executions to an agent it is not, or the runtime role moves the link | A binding is written only by `admin_bind_agent` and `admin_unbind_agent` under the 0021 actor check, joining an active AGENT principal to an agent this tenant's connection still lists, one to one by partial unique indexes; the runtime role can write neither the table nor `monitor_agents.linked_principal_id`; the page and `link-agent` append `agent.bound` and `agent.unbound` in the same transaction; unbinding takes a reason and withdraws the ExecBound reach rows at once; nothing infers a binding from a name (`migrations/versions/0037_agent_bindings.py`, `bindings.py`). | `tests/test_agents_bindings.py`, `tests/test_console_api_agents.py`, `tests/test_browser_console_directory.py` | Verified |
| T5.14 | E | A sandbox outlives the fourteen days it was granted, someone who is not an administrator of it extends, revives or converts it, or an abusive sandbox cannot be ended before its own next boundary | The end is a property of the row, kept by a trigger at the claim plus fourteen days for every writer, and moved by `extended_until`, `deactivated_at`, `converted_at` and, since `0044_hosted_free`, `fell_back_at` (T5.21 below). Day fourteen's default is now `sandbox_fallback`, which sets `plan = 'free'` and leaves the tenant active rather than ending it; `sandbox_expire` and `sandbox_notice` both refuse a slot that has already fallen back, so the two endings are mutually exclusive in both orders, and `sandbox_notice` refuses a second notice regardless. Extending, reviving or converting a sandbox happens only over the owner connection, by the hosted service's own commands (documented with the hosted service), each with its own bounds; each requires an active ADMIN principal of that tenant as the actor and appends `tenant.extended`, `tenant.fell_back` or `tenant.converted` to that tenant's own chain in the same transaction. Deactivation (T5.23 below) ends any claimed, unconverted sandbox at once regardless of `fell_back_at` and regardless of its end date, and refuses a converted tenant. Deactivation, by either path, is an inactive tenant, so sessions, credentials and agent requests fail at their next check as for any inactive tenant; a fallen-back tenant stays active and only its plan and cap change (`migrations/versions/0039_signups.py`, `migrations/versions/0044_hosted_free.py`, and the hosted plugin's own code). | hosted: OS7, OS7b, OS7c, OS7d, HF10, HF17, HF18, HF20, HF21, HF24 to HF30, HF46 | Verified |
| T5.15 | E | A console administrator gives themselves or somebody else an administrator's way in | `admin_set_email` and `admin_reset_password` carry the 0021 actor check and refuse an ADMIN target, so an administrator's sign-in address and set-password code stay with owner tooling exactly as their credentials do; an address another person holds anywhere is refused by a global unique index; the runtime role holds no write on `principals.email`, `principal_passwords`, `password_resets` or `principal_identities`, and `check_runtime` asserts the whole function catalog and the absence of those grants. `password_replace` refuses a person who holds no password, so the change route can never become a way to set one, and it takes the principal from the session rather than from a body (`migrations/versions/0040_sign_in.py`, `surfaces/people_pages.py`, `surfaces/console_api/settings.py`). | `tests/test_sign_in_store.py`, `tests/test_console_api_people.py::test_the_console_refuses_an_administrator_an_address_or_a_code`, `tests/test_console_api_settings.py::test_a_person_with_no_password_cannot_set_one_through_the_change_route` | Verified |
| T5.16 | E | A space membership raises a role, or the tenant is left with nobody who can administer spaces | The space role narrows and only narrows: a person's authority is `min(principals.kind, space role)`, `space_assign` refuses the `ADMIN` role for a `HUMAN_APPROVER` principal, and `principals.kind` stays a necessary condition wherever it is checked today, so no area module changes its authority. A tenant administrator is an active `ADMIN` principal with no membership, and the assignment that would leave a tenant with none is refused `LAST_TENANT_ADMIN` under a lock on the tenant's `ADMIN` rows, the way `revoke_executor_key` refuses a tenant's last live key (`migrations/versions/0041_spaces.py`). | SP3; `tests/test_spaces.py`, `tests/test_admin_cli.py` | Verified |
| T5.17 | R | A space and the history it owned are removed | There is no delete path: spaces archive, memberships are removed but the rows they governed keep their space, and the runtime holds no `DELETE` or `TRUNCATE` on either new table — `SELECT` and nothing else on `spaces` and `space_members`, and `INSERT` plus three update columns on `space_policy_selection` alone. An archived space keeps every row it owned in every view that could see it, and archiving the tenant's last unarchived space is refused (`migrations/versions/0041_spaces.py`, `db.py`). | SP9, SP10; `tests/test_spaces_schema.py` | Verified |
| T5.18 | E, I | A tenant's administrator registers an issuer they control, or a client secret and refresh token at rest are read | A provider an administrator registers can mint access for nobody on its own: a subject needs a link, a link needs that administrator's invitation, their direct link or the person's own self-link, and `directory_link` refuses an `ADMIN` target exactly as `admin_set_email` does, so administrator creation and cross-administrator links stay with the owner connection. A draft is verified only by a workspace administrator's sign-in through it: the self-link route refuses anybody else unless the provider is enabled, `directory_store.link_self` refuses the same in its transaction before `directory_link_self` runs, and since `0073_directory_workspace_admin` the function refuses it too, where before it accepted an approver's (T5.31). The worst a hostile issuer buys is approver access for accounts in a directory that administrator already controls, which issuing approver credentials already buys them. At rest, the client secret and the refresh token are AES-256-GCM ciphertext under a key ring in the private configuration, with the tenant, the provider and the purpose as additional authenticated data, so a row moved to another tenant, another provider or the other column does not open; a key that left the ring is named rather than skipped. The key is never in the database, the runtime role holds `SELECT` and no write on all four tables, and neither value is logged, rendered, returned by any function or present in an audit payload. What this does **not** defend is a process that can already read the gateway's configuration, which is where the connector tokens and the assistant key already are (`envelope.py`, `directory_store.py`, migration `0043_directory_sign_in`). | `tests/test_envelope.py::test_a_ciphertext_moved_to_another_row_or_column_does_not_open`, `tests/test_directory_store.py::test_an_administrator_cannot_be_linked_by_anybody_but_themselves`, `tests/test_directory_store.py::test_the_runtime_role_cannot_write_a_directory_table`, `tests/test_console_api_sign_in.py::test_a_registration_is_validated_against_the_directory_and_lands_as_a_draft`, `tests/test_console_api_directory_identity.py::test_an_approver_cannot_start_a_link_through_a_provider_that_is_not_enabled`, `tests/test_console_api_directory_identity.py::test_link_self_refuses_a_non_administrator_on_a_draft_and_writes_nothing` | Verified |
| T5.19 | E | Access here outlives an account being disabled at the customer's directory | Before a directory session is renewed, and only then, the refresh token is redeemed at the stored token endpoint outside every database transaction. `invalid_grant`, or an ID token whose subject or `tid` changed, revokes the credential and every session under it on that request, which is what both Entra ID and Okta answer for a disabled, deleted or session-revoked account. A session renews once half its fifteen minutes has passed, so a person using the console is rechecked about every seven and a half minutes and one who walked away is gone in fifteen. Every other error word, `invalid_client` included, is unavailability rather than a refusal, so a client secret rotated at the customer's end does not end every session in the tenant: the session is accepted while the last verification is within fifteen minutes and refused after it, and nothing is revoked, so an outage that resolves lets people carry on. Disabling the provider and unlinking a person both revoke every credential under them in the same function. **The residual is the window**: a person disabled at the directory keeps the console for up to the recheck interval, and one disabled during a directory outage keeps it for up to the grace; an administrator deactivating them is immediate (`directory_store.recheck_session`, `surfaces/human.py`). | See R26; `tests/test_directory_store.py::test_a_refused_recheck_ends_the_session_on_this_request`, `tests/test_directory_store.py::test_an_unreachable_directory_is_tolerated_and_then_refuses`, `tests/test_directory_store.py::test_a_disabled_provider_refuses_a_sign_in_and_ends_every_session_under_it` | Partial, see R26, R36 |
| T5.20 | E | A future change reads a workspace's agent limit or its agent-principal count anywhere on the request path, turning an administrative entitlement (the limit Hosted Free sets from its plan) into a security control that can fail open on a bug or block a legitimate incident response | The cap is evaluated only where an allowance can be taken, both of them administrative definer functions, and raises `PLAN_LIMIT` there alone: `admin_create_principal`'s own capped branch, and `admin_reactivate_principal` when its target is an agent a person deactivated by hand, because that deactivation freed an allowance and reactivating takes it back (without this, a workspace at its limit could deactivate one, create another and reactivate the first, holding one more, and repeat without limit). Both take `FOR NO KEY UPDATE` on the tenant row before counting live and sleeping agents against the same limit, the workspace's own `t.max_agents` (null is uncapped) since `0066_max_agents` replaced `0044_hosted_free`'s `CASE t.plan`, so a creation and a reactivation contending for the last allowance serialize on one lock and exactly one wins. Waking a sleeping agent is never refused, because a sleeping agent never stopped counting. `PLAN_LIMIT` is an administrative refusal that never appears in a policy decision's reasons, is never consulted by admission, evaluation, dispatch or approval, and changes nothing about an already-created principal's ability to act (an over-limit workspace keeps every agent it holds). The guarantee is checked structurally rather than by sampling routes: `execbound.requests` and every module under `execbound.execution`, `execbound.policy` and `execbound.resolution` are read as source text and asserted to contain neither the string `PLANS` nor `agent_principals` (HF53), and a wider walk that adds the agent surfaces, identity, the runtime, the Arcade hooks, the provider modules and the approval decision surfaces asserts that none names `max_agents`, `agent_capacity` or `execbound.capacity` (AC9), so a plan or limit lookup added to any walked module fails the suite closed rather than needing a reviewer to notice it (`capacity.py`, the hosted plugin's plan table, migrations `0044_hosted_free` and `0066_max_agents`). Modules outside the walk are not covered: `db.py`, whose `SCHEMA_REVISION` names the migration, and the shared helpers the walked modules import, such as `audit.py`, `operations.py`, `contracts.py`, `spaces.py` and `access_control.py`. | AC4, AC9; hosted: HF9, HF53, HF54 to HF58; `tests/test_agent_capacity.py` | Verified |
| T5.21 | E | A route reachable by a tenant, an agent or the public reaches `sandbox_fallback`, which (unlike `sandbox_expire`) leaves the tenant active rather than ending it, or the day-fourteen guard against an unclaimed, converted, already-deactivated or not-yet-due slot is missing from the function that replaced `sandbox_expire` as the scheduled pass's default | `sandbox_fallback(p_now)` is a fenced, no-actor `SECURITY DEFINER` function with exactly one caller, in the hosted plugin's own code: its scheduled signup pass, under its own fence (`execbound.tenant_id` set to the slot being processed and never to a caller-supplied value). No HTTP or MCP route, no agent-authenticated path and no console page calls it. The hosted service's own conversion command does **not** call this function: moving a sandbox to the free plan early produces the identical state (`plan = 'free'`, `fell_back_at` stamped, tenant left active) through its own direct `UPDATE`s on `execbound.tenants` and `execbound.onboarding_slots` over the fenced owner connection, a second, independent implementation of the same state change rather than a second caller of the function. `sandbox_fallback`'s guard restates `sandbox_expire`'s own: a slot that is unclaimed, converted, already deactivated, already fallen back or not yet due is `INVALID_SLOT`, and a clock more than five minutes from the database's is `INVALID_CLOCK`. `sandbox_expire` sets `tenants.active = false`; `sandbox_fallback` leaves it `true` and only lowers the tenant's cap from uncapped to three agent principals, a widening of what the tenant keeps relative to expiry's outcome and never a grant of authority the tenant did not already hold, and each refuses the state the other produces, so the two endings are mutually exclusive in both orders (migration `0044_hosted_free.py`, and the hosted plugin's own code). | hosted: HF10, HF17, HF18, HF46; `tests/test_route_authority.py` | Verified |
| T5.22 | I | Computing which free tenant's agents have gone ninety days silent reads audit event payloads, or the derived signal cannot be reproduced once a retention pass redacts payload content | `agents_sleep`'s own SQL and the hosted plugin's scheduled pass, which applies the same rule to choose the tenants it puts to sleep, both read only `audit_events.kind` (filtered to `execution.%`) and `occurred_at`; neither reads `payload`, so no retention or custody boundary is crossed to compute dormancy. Silence is measured from the later of the slot's own `claimed_at` and `max(occurred_at)` over the tenant's `execution.*` events, so a retention pass that redacts payload content and keeps every event's `kind` and `occurred_at` leaves it reproducible; no retention pass is built. A tenant with no `execution.*` event at all is judged by its own claim's age rather than treated as silent the instant it becomes free (migration `0044_hosted_free.py`, and the hosted plugin's own code). | hosted: HF33, HF36, HF40, HF41 | Verified |
| T5.23 | E | Deactivation, the operator's own way to end an abusive sandbox tenant at once regardless of its end date, becomes reachable from the runtime role or a tenant-facing route, handing an undated any-tenant off switch to the request path | Unlike `sandbox_expire`, `sandbox_fallback` and every other lifecycle function in this family, deactivation is deliberately **not** a `SECURITY DEFINER` function: `check_runtime()` requires every function in that catalog (`db.py`'s `FUNCTIONS`) to be executable by the runtime role, and an off switch reachable by the runtime role is an off switch reachable from the request path however carefully its own guard is written. Deactivation instead runs as a direct owner write in a fenced transaction on the owner connection, the same shape the hosted service's conversion already has: it locks the slot row, checks `claimed_at`/`converted_at`/`deactivated_at`, updates `tenants.active` and `onboarding_slots.deactivated_at` and appends `tenant.deactivated`, reachable from nowhere but the hosted plugin's own owner command, under the operator's own credential. `sandbox_deactivate` is asserted absent from `db.py`'s `FUNCTIONS` catalog by name, so a future change that turned it back into a definer function would fail this guard closed (`db.py`, and the hosted plugin's own code). | hosted: HF8, HF24, HF25, HF28 | Verified |
| T5.24 | E | A personal access token activates a policy, switches the workspace to observe-only, lifts an emergency stop, reactivates an agent, unarchives a space or installs or refreshes the sample targets | `token_rules.TOKEN_REFUSED` carries `/policy/{bundle_id}/activate`, `/tenant/resume`, `/agents/{principal_id}/reactivate`, `/spaces/{space_id}/unarchive` and `POST /samples` and `POST /samples/refresh`, which install and activate the starter bundle, not every method of `/samples`: `GET /samples`, which only reads the samples' status, is allowed to a token, as `/connection` is. `/tenant/mode` stays allowed, but `settings.py`'s `change_mode` route itself refuses a token's request unless the body's `mode` is `ENFORCE`, so `OBSERVE` is refused there rather than in the table; `TOKEN_BODY_LIMITS` carries only the reference text the published API describes that limit with. `/samples/remove`, which only removes what `/samples` installed, stays allowed because it tightens rather than loosens (`token_rules.py`, `surfaces/console_api/settings.py`). | PT3; `tests/test_console_api_tokens.py::test_a_token_may_switch_to_enforcing_and_not_to_observe_only`, `tests/test_console_api_tokens.py::test_the_exceptions_answer_token_not_allowed_and_are_audited`, `tests/test_console_api_tokens.py::test_a_token_reads_the_samples_status_its_owner_reads`, `tests/test_console_api_samples.py::test_sm9_api_a_token_may_not_refresh_the_samples` | Verified |
| T5.25 | E | A personal access token creates or changes administrators, people, access, groups, approval rules, sign-in providers or further personal access tokens | Every write under `/people`, `/access`, `/approvers`, approval rules and `/tenant/sign-in` is in `TOKEN_REFUSED`, as is every token-management route (`/settings/tokens*`, `/tenant/tokens*`); the hosted plugin's own invitation writes sit in its refused set, which `token_routes` registers: `personal_tokens.issue_token`, `revoke_token`, `delete_token` and `list_tokens` each refuse a token session outright before the store is reached, so a token can never mint, list, revoke or delete a token, its own or another's. `/credentials/issue`'s own body rule refuses a credential for any `HUMAN_APPROVER` or `ADMIN` principal, new or existing, and accepts only an agent, so a token cannot mint the one kind of credential a session can exchange for a sign-in; the ten-live-token ceiling (`admin_issue_personal_token`) bounds even an administrator's own issuing. This is what closes T4.43's approval-by-proxy case (`token_rules.py`, `personal_tokens.py`, migration `0063_personal_access_tokens`). | PT4, PT5; `tests/test_personal_tokens.py::test_a_token_cannot_manage_tokens`, `tests/test_console_api_tokens.py::test_a_token_issues_a_key_only_for_an_agent`, `tests/test_route_authority.py::test_token_allowed_writes_never_touch_people_access_or_sign_in`, `tests/test_personal_tokens.py::test_an_owner_holds_at_most_ten_live_tokens` | Verified |
| T5.26 | E | An administrator disabled at the workspace's identity provider keeps acting through a personal access token after a browser session would have re-verified and failed | A browser session can re-authenticate at the directory every fifteen minutes (`validate_workspace_session`'s recheck), so it is refused only once that recheck fails; a token never mints a `federated_sign_ins` row and has no way to prove itself there, so it is held to a stricter rule. `personal_tokens._directory_required` refuses issuing and using every token in the workspace the instant any identity provider row carries `require_sign_in`, in any state, rather than waiting for a recheck window to lapse (`DIRECTORY_REQUIRED`). | PT13; `tests/test_personal_tokens.py::test_a_token_is_refused_while_any_identity_provider_requires_sign_in`, `tests/test_console_api_tokens.py::test_directory_sign_in_refuses_a_token_as_it_refuses_a_session`, `tests/test_console_api_tokens.py::test_issuing_a_token_is_refused_where_directory_sign_in_is_required` | Verified |
| T5.27 | E | A rules bundle compiles its policy about another action than the one the mapping it names dispatches | Rules compile about a mapping's action, so the store refuses, at the ref's location (`mappings.N.action`), a `MappingRef.action`, or a rules bundle's `action_name` for a ref, that the tenant's mapping row does not serve; the row, not the bundle, decides what a ref dispatches, and evaluation denies a plan whose bundle names another action for its ref (`policy/store.py`, `policy/models.py`, `policy/evaluate.py`). | `tests/test_policy_store.py::test_a_mapping_naming_an_action_its_row_does_not_serve_is_refused_at_its_action`, `::test_a_rules_bundle_is_refused_where_its_ref_names_an_action_its_row_does_not_serve`, `tests/test_policy_evaluation.py::test_a_bundle_mapping_naming_another_action_than_the_plan_denies`, `tests/test_execution.py::test_rules_decide_a_request_on_a_ref_that_is_not_its_actions_name` | Verified |

On hosted the boolean describes an account-level factor the account shares across its workspaces, so it says the same thing in each workspace the person belongs to. An administrator's personal access token can read it through `GET /people`, as it can already read the people list. The same administrator can already require a factor for everyone (`account_security_policy`). | T5.38 | I | The operator's service-measures report re-identifies a workspace or a person, carries a workspace's records across the workspace boundary, or counts a workspace whose people refused analytics | The report is a hosted command over the owner connection that refuses any role but the schema owner (`OWNER_REQUIRED`) and appends nothing. It walks the directory through `job_tenants_page` after `check_directory_policy`, and reads each workspace in its own read-only, repeatable-read transaction fenced to that workspace, every statement naming the tenant. The reader returns a value holding only times, state words and booleans, so no identifier, name, address, destination or request number can reach the aggregation, and the output's keys are a fixed set. A workspace where anyone has recorded a refusal or withdrawal of analytics consent contributes to no figure, and neither does the showcase; the walk prints no count of either. A figure drawn from fewer than five workspaces, a count under five and a count whose complement is under five print as null and are named as suppressed, and so does any figure that could be worked out from printed ones: partitions print whole or not at all, a daily series prints whole or not at all beside its total, and origins are disjoint per run. The threshold is a constant, not an argument. Nothing is stored or shown on a product page. The owner role can still read every row directly, and can difference two runs over different windows, which the assumption that the owner is trusted already covers (the hosted plugin's own code). | hosted: MS3, MS4, MS5, MS6, MS7, MS8, MS9 | Verified |
| T5.39 | I | The dormancy warning reaches someone who is not a live administrator of the workspace, or an administrator's address reaches an audit event, a log line or the warning's own row (hosted service only) | Recipients are chosen inside the workspace's fence when the warning is queued (an active person, not an agent or the scheduler principal, holding workspace-wide `access.manage`, the test `agents_sleep` picks its actor by) and checked again in the same fence immediately before the send, so a person deactivated or narrowed in between is skipped (`NOT_ADMINISTRATOR`). The address is resolved at send time, from the account's address through an active membership or else the principal's own (`weekly_summary.resolve_address`), and is held only in memory for the send: the row has no column for it, nothing is appended to the audit chain, and the log lines carry the tenant, the warning's id, a count and a code. A person with no address is skipped (`NO_ADDRESS`) rather than sent anywhere else (the hosted plugin's own code). | hosted: DW2, DW6, DW7 | Verified |
| T5.40 | T | The dormancy warning tells an administrator something false (a sleep date after a governed request moved it, a sleep already reached, a workspace that is not approaching sleep) or carries something a mail client or a reader would act on (hosted service only) | The warning is queued only when `dormancy_warn` finds the workspace active, `free`, with an awake agent and between day 76 and day 90 of silence, and names the approach by the moment silence ran from. Before each send the workspace is read again in its fence, and the warning is skipped (`NO_LONGER_DUE`) unless it is still due on the same anchor and its sleep moment has not passed; a governed request starts a new approach with its own warning, and scheduler events are not activity. Sleep itself is never tied to a warning's delivery: a workspace sleeps at day 90 whether its warning was delivered, deferred and never sent, held by a drain or failed, and the sleep pass leaves the warning as it found it. The email is a fixed plain-text template whose only reader-supplied text is the workspace name through `safe_label`; it links only the console's origin and carries no token, tenant id or unsubscribe. The name is the workspace's own administrators' text, so it can still read as a web address, as the weekly summary's can (T4.53) (the hosted plugin's own code). | hosted: DW1, DW4, DW5, DW6, DW12 | Verified |
| T5.41 | D | A free workspace's administrators spend the operator relay's daily allowance with dormancy warnings, so every workspace's password reset and signup verification mail waits past its link's life, or one workspace's warnings hold back another's (hosted service only) | A workspace may make any number of administrators with addresses of its choosing, so the warnings are bounded by the relay rather than by the workspace. Warnings, signup mail and reset mail spend one daily allowance and each counts all three, a warning that a drain holds under a live lease counting with the delivered ones. A warning is sent only while the day's relay mail is below half of `daily_limit`, rounded down, and is leased only while the day's warnings, delivered or being sent, are below that half, read under one transaction-scoped advisory lock held until the lease is committed, so two drains running at once cannot both take the last of it: however many warnings are queued and however many drains run, the day's warnings as the service counts them never pass that half, and signup and reset mail keep the rest of the day as it is counted. That count is of mail recorded as delivered or held under a live lease, not of what the relay received. A warning whose delivery was uncertain because its drain died mid-send holds no room once its lease lapses, so it never blocks its own retry: the next pass takes it again, checks it afresh and sends it, counting it once whatever the relay received the first time, as a signup or reset email in that state is, and a fifth attempt left that way is failed, never sent a sixth time. So each send cut off after the relay took its message and before it was settled leaves the relay holding one message more than the day's count: on a day with such a send, warnings can reach the relay more often than the half allows and signup and reset mail can have less than the other half of the relay's own allowance, as a signup or reset email cut off the same way already runs the count short. A warning over that share waits for a later pass with its attempts untouched. Due warnings are handed out one workspace at a time, every workspace's first before any workspace's second, so a workspace with many administrators cannot spend the warnings' half before another workspace's turn, and within each turn the soonest sleep goes first. Warnings are best effort and sleep never waits for one (T5.40): a warning still waiting when its workspace's sleep moment arrives is skipped, never sent late, so a backlog larger than the share can carry before those moments leaves some administrators unwarned (`migrations/versions/0092_dormancy_warning.py`, and the hosted plugin's own code). | hosted: DW6, DW11 | Verified |

### B6: Monitoring sources and notification receivers

| ID | STRIDE | Threat | Mitigation | Evidence | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| T6.1 | S | Forged or replayed story events | Source tokens bound to one tenant, source and story; agent and ADMIN tokens are rejected at intake; duplicate receipts are idempotent. | T6.20 | D | An approver is not told of a live approval because the drain judged it lapsed, or because a retry or a cap deferral was dropped | The drain reads the deadline from the stored approval in the claim itself, never from a caller, and closes a row unsent as `LAPSED_BEFORE_DELIVERY` only when that approval is still pending and expires within fifteen seconds (`notifications.LAPSE_MARGIN`); with more time left the row is sent as before. A retry or a cap deferral that would land later than that point fails the row at once with the failed attempt's own code (`RECEIVER_UNAVAILABLE`, `MAIL_REFUSED`, `RATE_LIMITED` and so on), so a broken receiver or relay stays visible as a failure and is never hidden under a lapse. Every outcome is a row code the `notifications` command lists, and the approval itself is untouched, so the console still shows it to every approver. The setup verifier and the Connections view call it, and the sidebar, Home and the report count through its SQL twin `OVERDUE_CONNECTIONS` at the same boundary, under the reader's space predicate; an overdue or non-reporting connection never takes the allow tone, and quiet means stale sources plus overdue connections everywhere (`monitoring/store.py`, `surfaces/console_api/monitoring_setup.py`) | MQ7; `tests/test_monitoring_freshness.py::test_mq7_every_surface_answers_the_same_for_every_case`, `tests/test_monitoring_freshness.py::test_mq7_no_surface_keeps_a_rule_of_its_own`, `tests/test_monitoring_freshness.py::test_mq7_overdue_and_source_counts_follow_the_space` | Verified |
| T6.23 | S, E | A follow-up message is read as authority: an `approved` outcome taken by a receiving automation as permission to act, a reminder read as extending the approval, or a follow-up spends the email cap an announcement needed | Neither follow-up carries a control: a reminder is the announcement's fixed copy again, sent only while the approval is pending and more than fifteen seconds from its deadline, and it never writes `expires_at` or anything else on the approval, so its window is the one the approval already had; it is queued only once half the window has passed with at least three minutes left, at most once per approval and destination by the same key, and routed by the stored request as the announcement is. An outcome message carries no control, and the decision stays on the authenticated approval page; its copy and the webhook documentation say it decides nothing, does not say whether the action ran and grants nothing, and only the kernel authorises a dispatch. Reminders and outcome messages are opt-in per destination, so a receiver that never reads `kind` never gets one, and a row whose message was switched off is settled `MESSAGE_OFF` unsent. At most one outcome per approval and destination is a schema fact (`approval_notifications_one_per_kind`), its outcome and time are fixed when it is queued, and two racing drains queue and send it once. | T6.25 | T | Two collection runs collect one connection at once, or one keeps writing after the connection is revoked, and corrupt its cursor or its outcome, or collect after the customer stopped it | Every run, scheduled or an operator's `collect-tines` or `collect-entra`, first takes the connection's lease through `collector_claim`, for at most fifteen minutes measured by the database's own clock, and a second claim while it runs is refused; every write the run makes to its connection row lands only while the row carries that lease and is active, and otherwise raises `LEASE_LOST` or `CONNECTION_REVOKED` and changes nothing; the steps that write no connection row check the lease on the locked row; revoking a connection clears its lease in the same statement; a lease that expired unfinished is recorded `INTERRUPTED` (`RUN_INTERRUPTED`) by the next claim. Residual: a run's outcome and its release commit separately, so a run stopped between the two leaves its lease to expire, and the next claim records `INTERRUPTED` over the outcome it had recorded; a release that fails for any reason but a lost lease fails that run too. | MC6; `tests/test_monitor_lease.py::test_mc6_two_claims_from_two_connections_admit_exactly_one`, `tests/test_monitor_lease.py::test_mc6_a_write_with_a_lease_the_connection_no_longer_carries_changes_nothing`, `tests/test_monitor_lease.py::test_mc6_revoking_a_connection_mid_run_clears_the_lease_and_stops_the_run`, `tests/test_monitor_lease.py::test_mc6_an_expired_lease_is_recorded_interrupted_then_claimed`, `tests/test_monitor_collect_due.py::test_mc7_an_operator_collection_waits_for_no_one_and_overlaps_no_one` | Verified |

| T6.27 | I | A gateway, database or backup compromise yields a Tines API key an administrator pasted in the console | The gateway holds the collector's public key alone (the runtime configuration's `scout.collector`); the pasted key lives in one request's memory and is sealed, libsodium's sealed box through PyNaCl, in an envelope binding it to its tenant, connection and kind before anything is written; the database keeps that ciphertext, the id of the collector key it was sealed to and the key's SHA-256, and the runtime inserts and updates neither sealed column (three definer functions write them, migration 0087). Only the collector's private key opens it, and that key sits in the collector's environment alone: on the hosted service, a secret no other service receives; on a Community installation, the jobs container's own read-only mount; never the gateway's. The collector refuses an envelope naming another row (`SEALED_KEY_MISMATCH`) and a key that is not the registered one (`KEY_MISMATCH`) before any request; a run reads the ciphertext again once it holds the connection's lease, so a replaced key is the one it sends. `collector-reseal` moves every sealed row in every active workspace to a new collector key with its fingerprint unchanged, only from the ciphertext it read, so a key replaced meanwhile is never overwritten, while the collector holds both keys (`--collector-key` given twice) so nothing is sealed to a key it lacks; a deactivated workspace's rows stay under the old key until it is reactivated (`monitoring/sealing.py`, `monitoring/connect.py`, `monitoring/due.py`). Residual: a compromised gateway sees the keys pasted while it is compromised, and a re-seal does not protect a key already sealed to an exposed collector key, which has to be replaced in Tines. | MC2, MC3, MC4; `tests/test_monitor_sealing.py::test_mc2_the_gateway_holds_the_public_key_only_and_cannot_open`, `tests/test_monitor_sealing.py::test_mc2_nothing_the_gateway_loads_imports_the_collectors_half`, `tests/test_monitor_sealing.py::test_mc3_a_ciphertext_copied_to_another_row_is_refused_and_never_sent`, `tests/test_monitor_sealing.py::test_mc4_reseal_moves_every_sealed_row_to_the_new_key_and_leaves_the_rest`, `tests/test_monitor_sealing.py::test_mc4_a_key_replaced_while_a_reseal_runs_is_never_overwritten`, `tests/test_monitor_sealing.py::test_mc4_during_a_rotation_the_collector_opens_a_key_sealed_to_either`, `tests/test_monitor_sealing.py::test_mc3_a_key_replaced_after_the_pass_read_its_connections_is_the_one_collected`, `tests/test_community_bundle.py::test_the_collectors_key_is_mounted_into_the_jobs_container_alone`; hosted: MC24 | Verified |
