| T13.16 | E | A host whose sensor stopped reporting reads `clean` | `clean` comes only from a source registered as attesting liveness. Validation and activation refuse a mapping to `clean` over any other source at its threshold's location, and refuse a source that does not attest liveness for a capability with no `no_detection` value, which leaves it nothing to report when it sees nothing; resolution re-checks the rows it read; and `signals/mapping.combine` refuses a combined `clean` when any record has `attests_liveness` false, which every frozen `SignalRecord` must state, so evaluation and replay refuse it over frozen records whatever bundle they apply (`policy/store.py`, `signals/resolve.py`, `signals/mapping.py`, `resolution/models.py`). Whether a source attests liveness is the owner's statement at registration. | SG14; `tests/test_signal_bundle_validation.py::test_clean_needs_a_source_that_attests_liveness`, `::test_a_capability_without_no_detection_needs_a_source_that_attests_liveness`, `tests/test_signal_resolution.py::test_clean_needs_a_source_that_attests_liveness`, `tests/test_signal_mapping.py::test_clean_needs_every_record_from_a_source_that_attests_liveness`, `tests/test_signal_evidence.py::test_a_record_must_say_whether_its_source_attests_liveness`, `tests/test_signal_evaluation.py::test_a_forged_clean_over_a_source_without_liveness_is_refused`, `tests/test_signal_replay.py::test_a_candidate_reading_clean_from_a_source_without_liveness_cannot_be_read` | Verified, see R31 |
| T13.17 | E | Resolution's reading of the records is trusted without a check | Evaluation re-applies the evaluated bundle's own mapping, through the same `combine`, to the records the plan froze, and denies a plan whose value, source, source version, object, observation or validity they do not reproduce, or whose records were observed or received after the decision (`policy/evaluate.py`). | SG10; `tests/test_signal_evaluation.py::test_a_fact_its_records_do_not_reproduce_is_denied`, `::test_an_observation_its_records_do_not_reproduce_is_denied`, `::test_validity_beyond_the_producers_expiry_is_denied`, `::test_a_record_observed_after_the_decision_is_refused`, `::test_a_record_received_after_the_decision_is_refused`, `::test_a_plan_is_invalid_under_a_bundle_that_reads_its_records_differently` | Verified |
| T13.18 | E | A mistaken or compromised detector, anyone able to raise a `Medium` alert on a host, or a thief holding a live producer token makes an isolation skip review | Not prevented. A record authenticates its source, not its verdict, and the mapping is applied as written, so one admitted source's `compromised` satisfies the no-review rule for any inventoried host, on a request from an agent inside that source's principal scope. It is bounded by the policy's forbids (a domain controller is denied whatever the EDR says), the source's principal scope, the token's lifetime (at most 720 hours from issue, which a rotation's grace never extends), immediate revocation, and isolation being reversible. | `tests/test_signal_execution.py::test_a_compromised_host_is_isolated_without_review`, `tests/test_signal_evaluation.py::test_a_domain_controller_is_denied_whatever_the_edr_says`, `tests/test_signal_registration.py::test_revocation_is_immediate_and_final`, `::test_the_grace_never_outlasts_the_old_tokens_expiry` | Accepted, see R30 |

## Residual risks

| ID | Risk | Where stated |
| --- | --- | --- |
| R1 | Tenant isolation relies on trusted runtime code setting the tenant context. Anyone with arbitrary SQL access and the runtime credential can change that context; that attacker is outside the isolation guarantee. | [README.md](../README.md) design authority; [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md) trust boundaries |
| R2 | A privileged writer who replaces both the audit history and the retained head defeats chain verification. Independently retained checkpoints are the intended detection; the restore verifier compares a restored chain against them, and `audit-verify` places them on the live chain every hour where they are configured (on Community, a copy in `private/checkpoints/`, which catches a change made through the database alone, since the host's owner can replace it), but keeping the originals off the database host and scheduling backups remain the operator's responsibility. | [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md); [RECOVERY.md](RECOVERY.md) |
| R3 | Provider HMACs establish authenticity between trusted components sharing a key, not third-party nonrepudiation. A security signal is authenticated by its producer's bearer token and carries no producer signature, so its frozen digest shows what ExecBound admitted and under which registration version, which answers a producer's dispute only for a party that trusts ExecBound's audit chain (T13.6). | [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md); [REPLAY.md](REPLAY.md) |
| R4 | The process boundary between the gateway, providers and replay is an application capability boundary, not an independently administered operating-system sandbox. A configured gateway process and its cross-tenant database credential remain trusted. | [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md); [SURFACES.md](SURFACES.md) |
| R5 | A bundle whose writer allowlist admits a label the protected agent can write removes the scenario T protection against agent-written context. | [CONTEXT_CONTRACTS.md](CONTEXT_CONTRACTS.md); [POLICY.md](POLICY.md) |
| R6 | The Cedar engine is a pinned community binding, used through private entry points because the public wrapper hides missing diagnostics. Every upgrade is a review item. | [POLICY.md](POLICY.md); `tests/test_cedar.py::test_pinned_native_entry_points_keep_their_shape` |
| R7 | External monitoring is detective and cooperative. It cannot prevent execution, a heartbeat proves reporting freshness rather than complete coverage, and a total ExecBound outage cannot self-alert. | [TINES_MONITORING.md](TINES_MONITORING.md) |
| R8 | Failed authentication is counted per surface, reason, minute and verified subject, and `execbound failures` lists the counters; requests refused by the request limits are not counted, the counters carry no caller address or raw token, no alerting reads them, and the limits themselves remain per process rather than distributed. | [EXECUTION.md](EXECUTION.md); [OPERATIONS.md](OPERATIONS.md#expiry-rotation-and-retention) |
| R9 | Key rotation reloads each process's private file independently, so a multi-process deployment can briefly verify with different rings; provider dispatch and control tokens still need a restart to change; and a key removed while an operation is unsettled leaves it uncertain until the key is restored. Backup, restore and verification are documented and rehearsed in CI on synthetic data, not yet on a real deployment. | [OPERATIONS.md](OPERATIONS.md); [RECOVERY.md](RECOVERY.md) |
| R10 | Authority is a bounded snapshot. Registry and credential changes are observed by fresh reads, but an independent writer can change them after the final read. Incorrect trusted provisioning is outside the agent input boundary. | [EXECUTION.md](EXECUTION.md) |
| R11 | One hosted deployment has been validated: the hosted service's pilot of September 16 to 18, 2026, with synthetic data and mock providers, TLS terminated by its host and the database reached with a pinned CA bundle. It is a pilot, not a production deployment, and named host trials are unperformed. | [DEPLOYMENT.md](DEPLOYMENT.md#validated-deployments) |
| R12 | One real binding exists, Microsoft Graph's user disable, and it ran against a lab directory only; its precondition is a read and comparison with a measured window, not atomicity (T3.15). Every other binding is a controlled mock: CrowdStrike, Defender and the remaining Entra operations, and their precondition and journal guarantees at the real vendor, are unverified. | [PROVIDER.md](PROVIDER.md); [ACTION_CATALOG.md](ACTION_CATALOG.md#vendor-reference) |
| R13 | Cutting off a compromised executor key leaves a grant only that key could have attested with no **attested** exit: every report carrying a signed record, the administrator's settlement and a replacement key are refused at the same verifier. A grant nobody reports at all keeps its reservation and its canonical claim indefinitely, because the sweep leaves the row `INDETERMINATE` and the write-off that would close it — releasing such a claim without settling the execution and audited as its own administrator act — does not exist. A grant whose holder still has its report secret is now a different case and a worse one: the bare word settles it, the impact is confirmed, the recovery run frees the target, and the correction of that settlement is permanently unreachable in that tenant, so the cut-off ends with charged, closed, uncorrectable rows where it used to leave open ones. | [CHECKPOINT.md](CHECKPOINT.md); [ARCADE.md](ARCADE.md); [OPERATIONS.md](OPERATIONS.md#register-and-cut-off-executor-keys) |
| R14 | The per-principal cap bounds unsettled grants, not held claims. A settlement on an executor's bare word leaves the unsettled set and frees a cap slot while its canonical claim is still held, so between that settlement and the next recovery run past the act window one principal can hold more live claims than the cap admits. Closing it means counting held claims at admission. | [CHECKPOINT.md](CHECKPOINT.md) |
| R15 | A precondition class is a statement about a vendor's API that ExecBound cannot verify, and an `UNAVAILABLE` binding makes a concurrent change undetectable. No vendor's conditional write, version semantics or journal behaviour has been exercised: the whole checkpoint path is fixture-verified against the protected mocks, and the live half of its workflow contract has not been run. | [CHECKPOINT.md](CHECKPOINT.md); [SURFACES.md](SURFACES.md#private-configuration-and-startup) |
| R16 | A Gate caller holds a vendor credential and a protected agent holds none, so one principal should serve Gate mappings or Execute mappings and not both. Nothing in the code enforces that separation: admission refuses a Gate mapping whose account already has a connector, which keeps one operation from being both, and says nothing about a principal holding one mapping of each kind. Owner provisioning is the only control today. | [CHECKPOINT.md](CHECKPOINT.md); [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md) trust boundaries |
| R17 | The Arcade correlation table holds a **usable** report secret while a grant is live, which is a property this carrier introduces rather than inherits: a Gate secret is returned and never stored, and only its SHA-256 digest is kept. A reader of that table can settle the row it names and, once the act window has passed, cause the scheduled run to release its canonical claim. The secret authenticates nothing past the report window, and it is nulled at settlement and cleared opportunistically afterwards, so what is left is the bytes of a row nobody touches again. No reader of it can reduce what the tenant is charged. | [ARCADE.md](ARCADE.md); [CHECKPOINT.md](CHECKPOINT.md) |
| R18 | An ungoverned toolkit is a standing operator decision with no record behind it: its calls create no execution, no reservation and no audit event, so neither the tenant's hash chain nor the correlation table can say how many passed, and tools the vendor adds to that toolkit later pass under the same decision. A declared tool is the opposite trade — it fails closed on vendor schema drift, so an added optional input breaks every governed call until an operator declares it. No coverage may be claimed for an ungoverned toolkit, and its enforcement mode stays `Unknown` even where the governed tools are later qualified. | [ARCADE.md](ARCADE.md); [INTEGRATIONS.md](INTEGRATIONS.md#enforcement-modes) |
| R19 | A settlement at `FAILED_EFFECT_UNKNOWN` **overcharges a call that did nothing**: the state says the effect was never established, and the impact is charged as though it had been, for the length of each limit rule's own window. That is deliberate, because the alternative is a refund on nobody's evidence, and it is what stops a single failed tool call wedging the intent permanently. The way back is an administrator signing a non-execution for that one row, which is a human act and not an automatic refund, and which is unavailable in a tenant whose executor key ring has been revoked as compromised. | [ARCADE.md](ARCADE.md); [CHECKPOINT.md](CHECKPOINT.md) |
| R20 | The correction is a per-row human act, and **ExecBound cannot tell a false report from a true one**. The Activity page offers an ADMIN the control on a correctable row and the kernel checks the record against a registered key, so what is verified is that the executor signed a non-execution for this operation — never that the platform's earlier statement was a lie. Whatever established that is evidence an operator gathered outside ExecBound, one row at a time, and a tenant facing many falsely reported failures corrects them one at a time or carries the charge. `execbound reconcile` is not this exit: it looks the account up in the live connector inventory and refuses a grant in every mode. | [CHECKPOINT.md](CHECKPOINT.md); [OPERATIONS.md](OPERATIONS.md#settle-an-unreported-checkpoint-grant) |
| R21 | Little about a real Arcade deployment is established: one live trial on 18 September 2026 observed blocking twice and state end to end on one call ([ARCADE.md](ARCADE.md)). The hook contract was read from a **beta** published schema on one day; the failure mode, the hook timeout, whether another extension is registered and whether the project routes every tool through this one are all on Arcade's side and unreadable from here. Three wire facts in particular cost a settlement or a tool if the deployment differs: `success` is optional on the wire while every settlement rests on it, `execution_id` is unconstrained on the wire and bounded here, and a `content-type` framed otherwise is a 400 the published contract does not retry. | [ARCADE.md](ARCADE.md) |
| R22 | A gateway compromise could mint invitation codes for free hosted sandboxes and mail them to an attacker's addresses. The prize is a mock-backed sandbox tenant with no real connector in it, bounded by `signups.capacity` and by the per-domain and global caps; every invitation is single use, and a claimed sandbox is never handed out again. The operator's signup report shows sign-ups diverging from the sandboxes taken. Nothing about it reaches a customer tenant. | T1.12, T4.18 |
| R23 | **A space is ownership and view, not isolation.** A compromise of the gateway, or arbitrary SQL with the runtime credential, reaches every space of a tenant exactly as it reaches every row of that tenant today. No document, page or sentence may describe a space as a boundary; the tenant is the boundary and the discipline is the one already applied to `ENFORCED_BY_PROVIDER` against `CHECKED_BY_CONNECTOR`. | [SURFACES.md](SURFACES.md); [OPERATIONS.md](OPERATIONS.md) |
| R24 | A tenant-wide limit and the canonical target claim are **cross-space denial and inference channels by design**: a tenant-scoped rule exhausted by space A denies space B, and a claim held in A refuses B's execution on the same canonical target with the conflict it returns today. B learns that something in the tenant is holding that target; the refusals say nothing about which space or what, and no page names another space's rows. A per-customer ceiling is the `space`-scoped limit rule. | [POLICY.md](POLICY.md); SP7, SP8 |
| R25 | The space predicate has **no database backstop**. A missing one is a correctness bug with a customer-visible consequence rather than a refusal, and the isolation test is the whole of the mitigation (T2.9). It reads the tables `0041_spaces.py` names in `OWNED_TABLES` through the shared predicate, and it holds every other table the catalog shows carrying a space to a case of its own, so a table given a space later fails it until somebody writes one; what it cannot do is write the case. Those other cases are negative tests of the readers they name, not of every reader a table has: a role in `access_role_grants` (0047) through the approval queue, the approval, Activity, Home, the visible set and the decision; a rule in `approval_rules` (0048) through the queue, the approval, the decision and the notification; a source in `signal_sources` (0068) through resolution and bundle validation (T13.12), and through the label the approval and execution pages name it by; an agent's `principals` row and its space's policy selection through admission and the action listing; and an agent's `principals` row and its keys' identifiers and status through the administrator's credentials page (T5.29). The signal source labels the console and the classic approval page show take the shared predicate too, over the visible set the record they belong to is read under (`human.signal_source_labels`): a row the reader may see whose frozen plan names a source of a space they cannot see, and which is not offered tenant-wide, names the fact by its kind and nothing of that source. The record's frozen plan is still shown as it froze: an administrator narrowed to spaces reads its signal records, each with its source's id and producer name, though never that source's label. Since `0071_space_credential_scope`, the credentials page reads only the administrator's spaces: an agent and its keys' identifiers and status take the shared predicate, as the Agents page's do (`admin_pages.credentials_overview`). Since `0072_space_principal_scope`, what an administrator may change has a predicate of its own beside the visible set, `spaces.administered_clause`, the rule the definer functions apply (T5.29, T5.30): the credentials page's people, the People list and a person's page, the workspace's token list and the console's `require_agent` and `require_person` take it, so a narrowed administrator is shown only the people they may change, and a change to an agent they may not change answers 404. A missing one shows them a target they cannot change and the function still refuses the write, so here the database does back the write and only the read has no backstop. Lists that only name people stay unfiltered on purpose: the approvers page's assign form and assignments, and the approvers an approval names. | SP1, SP15, SP18; `tests/test_spaces_isolation.py::test_every_space_carrying_table_has_a_case`, `tests/test_signal_views.py::test_a_label_of_another_spaces_source_is_withheld_from_a_reader_of_this_one`, `::test_a_source_offered_tenant_wide_keeps_its_label_in_every_space`, `::test_a_member_of_the_sources_space_reads_its_label_whatever_space_they_look_at` |
| R26 | Deprovisioning at a customer's directory reaches ExecBound on a poll rather than a push, so access here outlives a disabled account by up to the recheck interval, and by up to the fifteen-minute grace while the directory is unreachable. Neither Entra ID nor Okta pushes an event this relying party can receive today: Entra transmits no Shared Signals event to an outside application at all, and redeeming a refresh token is the one mechanism both honour. A Shared Signals receiver and SCIM provisioning shorten this and are Enterprise (T5.19). | T5.19; `tests/test_directory_store.py::test_an_unreachable_directory_is_tolerated_and_then_refuses` |
| R27 | A Tines ES256 signing credential cannot be host-restricted, because Tines refuses a credential in a cryptographic function while domain restriction is enabled; any story in the team holding it can produce a valid attestation, and the checkpoint's own report secret is readable in the same team's event data for the life of a grant. The recipe's answer is a dedicated Tines team and short event retention, an operator practice rather than a control the protocol enforces. | [CHECKPOINT.md](CHECKPOINT.md) |
| R28 | The sample key is one secret for every workspace's sample connection. Whoever holds it can act on every workspace's sample endpoint mock as that workspace's sample account, and on nothing else: the mock holds synthetic targets only and nothing real is derived from the key. Rotating it is a restart of the gateway and the endpoint mock that cuts every sample connection at once, and there is no rotation from the console. A sample target's facts are recorded at install and go stale after the starter ruleset's hour; an administrator's refresh (`POST /api/console/v1/samples/refresh`) records them again by removing and adding the samples in one transaction, which gives each target a new incident. The refresh is refused, changing nothing, and the facts stay stale while a request on the samples is unresolved, a request whose approval lapsed included until something ends it; while a bundle a person made names the sample account, as a copy of the starter from Set your controls' copy of the active policy or its templates does, until it is retired; while a space other than the default one selects the starter; and once the workspace has a policy or a connection of its own. The way past the second is to retire that bundle in Policies, activating the starter again first where it is the active one, since a selected bundle cannot be retired and the refresh's install refuses any other active policy. | [PROVIDER.md](PROVIDER.md) |
| R29 | Until an export runs on the public repository, its CI workflow is held by tests rather than by a run, and until a version tag is pushed, so is its release workflow. Until then its shape is held by tests and not by a run, and the `release` environment, the tag ruleset, the `RELEASE_IMAGE` variable and the package's visibility are repository settings that no test reads (T12.6). What the development repository proves after every merge is the stages `scripts/ci.py` runs, not the YAML that calls them, and the bounds of the suite's timed tests, set on a quiet runner, are unmeasured on shared ones. The file-by-file review of an export before it is published is a person's step, not a check. | T12.1, T12.6 |
| R30 | A security signal proves which registered source sent it, never that its verdict is true. A false positive, a compromised EDR console or a stolen producer token is enforced as the mapping reads it, and in the signals guide's example scenario the enforced direction is permissive: `compromised` lets an isolation run without review, the most restrictive combination lets any one admitted source supply it, and Defender's `Medium` maps to it. A false `None` from the only admitted source sends a compromised host's isolation to review instead. | [SIGNALS.md](SIGNALS.md#evidence-and-limits); T13.18 |
| R31 | A signal source's placement, input path and liveness attestation are the owner's statements at registration, which ExecBound cannot observe. A source registered `independent` but fed by the agent's runtime lets a compromised runtime choose what the producer judges; one registered as attesting liveness that cannot show its sensor is still reporting lets a silent host read `clean`. The Defender for Endpoint producer is an EDR, independent by construction, and does not attest liveness, so it can never produce `clean`. | [SIGNALS.md](SIGNALS.md#evidence-and-limits); T13.3, T13.16 |
| R32 | Nothing prunes security signal records, so a source's 500,000-record cap counts its whole life: about 347 host-days at one report per host a minute, a week for fifty hosts, and about 14 hours for a thief at the rate limit. Then every envelope is refused `SOURCE_CAPACITY`, and every fact resting on the source is `INVALID_CONTEXT` until the owner registers a new source and activates a bundle admitting it. It fails closed. | [SIGNALS.md](SIGNALS.md#evidence-and-limits); T13.9 |
| R33 | Replay keeps each fact's validity as it was frozen, so a candidate bundle that shortens a signal's freshness replays as a certain `DENY` even where the record was fresh enough, and a certain denial leaves the what-if ledger, so later decisions can show more room than the candidate would have left them. A freshness change cannot be previewed over history; inventory facts behave the same way. | [REPLAY.md](REPLAY.md); `tests/test_signal_replay.py::test_rederive_reads_the_frozen_records_through_the_candidate` |
| R34 | Text the screens of T7.6 and T7.7 cannot reach. Audit events already written and bundle contents already stored are immutable, and a destination an earlier event failed stays failed: `export-resume` reads that event again and no command moves a cursor past a refused event (`export.py` `resume_export`), and the replay export refuses every export of that tenant the same way. Values the registry already holds pass no screen when new plans and events copy them: a resource's provider id and version, a mapping's operation ref and version and a manifest's values reach the frozen plan an `execution.transition` carries, an executor key id reaches `executor_key.revoked` or `executor_key.compromised` when it is named, the old id a rotation retires reaches `executor_key.rotated` as `previous_key_id` (`administration.py` `rotate_executor_key`, which screens only the new id), a context source's writer label reaches `context.imported`, and a hosted slot's seed reaches `tenant.claimed`; refusing them at resolution would deny every action on them. A policy bundle stored before the bundle screen existed keeps copying its rule ids into new `execution.evaluated` events for as long as it stays active: `policy/evaluate.py` derives `HARD_LIMIT:<rule id>` and `limit:<rule id>` from a bundle's own rule ids on every evaluation, and the bundle screen in `policy/store.py` `_insert_draft` reaches only a bundle written from now on, never one already stored. Operator configuration strings that reach an event, the assistant's `model` in `assistant.asked` and the hosted `consent_version` in `tenant.provisioned`, are deployment configuration and are not screened. A person whose stored address already has a domain of that shape cannot be invited again under it by `invite_person`, which fails closed. A slots file that already holds a seed of that shape fails closed only where the seed reaches an event: the hosted plugin's invitation writers refuse it before anything is written. Every other reader of the file reads it as it always has; a screen where the file is parsed would have stopped the service starting over a seed no write of theirs carries, so the screen sits at those writers instead. | T7.6, T7.7; [OPERATIONS.md](OPERATIONS.md), event export; [REPLAY.md](REPLAY.md) |
| R35 | Both Tines stories are Advisory, because nothing in Tines forces a story through ExecBound. The governed-action story is governed only because its author wrote the call: a caller holding its own vendor credential can skip it, and in its trial a story with no provider credential was refused by the provider, not by ExecBound. The Gate story leaves the vendor credential in the customer's Tines team, so any story there that holds it can call the vendor without asking, a bypass the Gate trial demonstrated, and what a story does with a grant, honouring a denial, acting once on the granted target and reporting truthfully, is its own cooperation. ExecBound decides, charges and records only what a story asks it; an action no story asked about leaves nothing here to find. | [INTEGRATIONS.md](INTEGRATIONS.md#enforcement-modes); [the Tines recipe](../integrations/tines/README.md); T8.10 |
| R36 | A directory outage locks people out of the console by design once T5.19's grace runs out. A directory session that cannot be rechecked stands for fifteen minutes past its last verification and is refused after that, and signing in again needs the same directory, so for the rest of the outage a person whose only sign-in is that directory, an approver included, can neither read the console nor decide an approval in it. Nothing is revoked, so an outage that resolves does not force a tenant-wide sign-in. Passwords and credentials keep working beside a directory, so a person who holds one is the way in during an outage; refusing them once everybody is linked is not built, and nor is a break-glass path for when it is. This is the availability side of T5.19, the opposite case to R26, where access outlives a disabled account. | T5.19; [OPERATIONS.md](OPERATIONS.md#give-a-person-a-password-and-set-up-a-sign-in-provider); `tests/test_directory_store.py::test_an_unreachable_directory_is_tolerated_and_then_refuses` |
| R37 | A Policy manager's preview, or a narrowed administrator's, replays the whole tenant's chain in server memory, because the export's integrity check needs the complete prefix. Only the decisions recorded in their Spaces leave it, but `complete` and `issues` are tenant-wide codes, and a candidate's limit differences count tenant-wide impact, as shared limit scopes intend, so a preview can reflect that other Spaces acted without naming what. | [REPLAY.md](REPLAY.md); `tests/test_policy_preview_spaces.py`; T5.35 |
| R38 | The value report's and the weekly summary's counts are counts of what ExecBound recorded, not of harm prevented: a refused request may have been benign and an executed one harmful. The report, the card and the summary say so in fixed copy. Observe mode's would-have counts are a simulation of the active bundle at the time and are never added to enforced counts. | [CONSOLE_GUIDE.md](CONSOLE_GUIDE.md); T4.53 |
| R39 | Text a person typed in an approval's comment rests in that browser tab's storage until a decision, a refusal that ends the approval, sign-out, the approval's deadline (swept when the console next loads the store) or the tab's closing, and a secret pasted by mistake rests there until the kernel refuses it when the decision is sent. It is never sent anywhere but that decision. | T4.59; `tests/test_browser_console_approvals.py::test_a_typed_comment_survives_signing_in_again` |
| R40 | Email control is account control for an account without a second factor: whoever reads its mailbox can reset its password on the hosted service. The reset ends every session, mails a notice and appears in the bell and in each workspace's audit chain, so the owner learns of it, but cannot stop it in advance except by enrolling a factor. For an enrolled account, mailbox control buys online guessing of the factor at a bounded rate (five guesses a link, ten links a day), and can set the factor's fifteen-minute lock up to ten times a day. Anyone who knows an account's address can spend its three reset emails an hour and ten a day, and someone who knows twenty addresses can spend the sixty an hour for everyone. The database call takes slightly longer or shorter with an account than without, which the network hides but the design does not equalise. | T4.81, T4.82 |
| R41 | A replay regression fixture is pseudonymized, not anonymized. It keeps the event sequence and every time, the number and kinds of decisions and their outcomes, catalog actions, contracts and writers, policy text, rule and obligation ids, enumerated fact values and claims, a signal fact's registration number, verdicts, producer names and versions and request numbers, and the counts of identifiers and labels it replaced, so someone who knows a tenant's history well can recognize it. A label whose text is also one of those kept words, or a token of one, is replaced where it was recorded and left as that word everywhere else, Cedar text included: the word is in the fixture anyway, but a copy of the label in free text keeps it. An operation ref or a version with no letter in it, such as `7` or `2026-10-01`, is replaced and looked for only as a whole value or JSON string, so a copy of one inside policy text stays as it is. Its integrity is internal consistency only: its head travels with it and its journals are re-signed under a published fixture key, so a fixture is regression evidence about the engine and never provenance. Only fixtures built from synthetic worlds are committed to this repository; one built from a real tenant is handled like the export it came from, and is written readable by its owner alone. Reading a fixture refuses one holding a secret, an identifier that is not a pseudonym or source text at a label position, but it cannot tell a tenant's free text typed by hand into policy text, such as a host named in a comment added to a committed candidate, from any other policy text, since nothing of the source travels with a fixture; such an edit rests on review of the pull request's diff, as re-recorded expectations do. A correctly built fixture of a real tenant reads like a synthetic one, so which world a committed fixture came from rests on review too. | [REPLAY.md](REPLAY.md#regression-fixtures); T7.8 |

## Assumptions

- The gateway process, its private configuration files and its database credential are trusted. Compromise of the host running them is outside this model.
- Owner provisioning is correct: canonical identities, approval assignments, inventory and incident sources, and signal source registrations (placement, input path and liveness) describe the real environment.
- The PostgreSQL administrator and the owner role are trusted; migrations run only through the owner connection.
- Approvers and administrators are the people their credentials were issued to. Credential issuance happens outside the gateway.
- In a deployment, TLS termination and origin configuration are set up as the operations guide requires; the secure cookie and HSTS behavior depend on it.
- Provider origins are HTTPS with a certificate the gateway verifies; cleartext is accepted only for a loopback IP address and there is no opt-in for private networks.

## Out of scope

- Denial of service against the host, network or PostgreSQL from outside the application.
- Vulnerabilities in the customer's agent platform, identity provider or SOAR system.
- Physical or hypervisor-level attacks.
- Social engineering of approvers outside the approval page.

## Maintaining this document

Update this document in the same PR whenever a change adds an HTTP route, MCP tool, human page, CLI command or monitoring route; adds a connector or external integration; adds a migration that touches authority tables; or closes an item marked Open or Partial. Keep threat and risk identifiers stable so issues and reviews can cite them. When an acceptance scenario is added, add its identifier to the row it evidences. A change that weakens a Verified row needs the failing test first, as the public repository's `CONTRIBUTING.md` requires for security behavior.

`tests/test_threat_model_references.py` checks what this section asks for, so a stale citation fails a run instead of waiting for a reviewer to click it: every cited test file exists and every cited node is defined in it, every acceptance identifier is a row or a group in the [acceptance inventory](ACCEPTANCE_TESTS.md), except one written after `hosted:`, which names a row of the hosted service's own inventory and is checked against it where a tree carries it and counted as evidence the tree cannot see where it does not, every document link resolves, every "see R<n>" names a residual risk that exists, each boundary table carries the six columns, each status is in the vocabulary above, and no Verified row leaves its evidence column empty. A row may cite a test in tests/hosted, which covers the hosted service's plugin, or in tests/internal, which covers files that are not published; a tree that holds the directory checks the citation like any other, and a tree without it, such as the self-hosted source tree, counts the citation as evidence it cannot see and neither fails nor skips it. It reads the document through the parser that also feeds `scripts/threat_model_index.py --next-ids`, so the helper an implementing pull request uses and the check it has to pass read the table the same way.

Take a new row's id from that helper rather than from the table, and take it again before merging. The helper answers with the highest id a boundary has used plus one, so two branches open at the same time will either choose the same number or leave one unused, the way two Alembic revisions collide on the same head. That is how B4 came to have no T4.8 and how three rows of B6 were numbered a second time. Because identifiers stay stable once published, the repair moved the later rows to T6.15, T6.16 and T6.17 rather than resequencing the boundary, and T4.8 stays unused for good. The check requires each boundary's ids to be unique and ascending rather than contiguous, and the helper names every unused and duplicated id, so a reader can tell a deliberate gap from a mistake.
